# FTC GEarheads (Team #16460) — Complete Documentation Archive

This document is a comprehensive, unabridged compilation of every file in the **context-for-robotics-2025** folder: FIRST Tech Challenge (FTC) team documentation for Team #16460, the GEarheads, of Brookfield, Wisconsin, spanning multiple seasons (2022-23 Power Play through 2025-26 DECODE). All text content below was extracted programmatically from the original PDF, Word, and PowerPoint files and is reproduced verbatim inside code blocks, organized by file type, season, and document category. Embedded images/diagrams that could not be extracted as text are flagged inline as `[IMAGE: description]`.

## Archive Contents at a Glance


| File Type | Count | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| PDF (.pdf) | 6 | Award Summary, Engineering Portfolio, Pit Posters, Questions, New Member Process, State Competition Posters |
| Word (.docx) | 6 | 2 are byte-identical duplicates (Judging Presentation / Judging Presentation (1)) |
| PowerPoint (.pptx) | 6 | 2 are byte-identical duplicates (Judging Presentation 2022-23 / Wisconsin State Competition Presentation 2022-23) |
| PNG screenshots | 2 | FIRST official events-site team history table (viewed and transcribed below, not OCR'd) |
| **Total files** | **20** | |

Design Highlights

                                    Intake System
                                    Our updated intake system rotates the GBV2 intake
                                    horizontally. This increases speed, efficiency, and
                                    accuracy. We have already seen huge
                                    improvements in our intake performance.

                                    Spindexer
                                    Our new spindexer has a much simpler design. It is
                                    fed directly from the intake. This prevents the robot
                                    from taking in four artifacts and allows the system to
                                    index much faster.

                                    Launcher
                                    After attending the Governor’s Cup, we discovered
                                    that using one flywheel instead of two improves
                                    launcher accuracy. With fewer moving parts, there
                                    are fewer factors that can cause error. However,
                                    different for this bot we needed to add a lifter
                                    mechanism to replace the helper fly wheels
                                    fromDBV2.

                                    Robot Control …                                                                                Return

  Optical Odometry
•Optical odometry sensors replace conventional
wheels for precise robot localization during
autonomous mode.
•These compact sensors track surface patterns,
providing high-resolution data on linear and rotational
motion.
•Improves navigation accuracy
•Enhances autonomous performance by offering
reliable feedback.



                                                Camera Based Trajectory Calculation
AprilTag-Based Goal Alignment: The robot uses AprilTag detection
to identify the scoring goal, autonomously orient itself, and calculate
the required launch power, enabling accurate shots from anywhere
on the field.
Autonomous Support in TeleOp: This AprilTag-based alignment and
launch calculation assist drivers during TeleOp by automatically
maintaining correct orientation and shot parameters, reducing
workload and improving consistency.                                        To achieve this, we use limelight cameras mounted along the side of the robot




  Driver Control Enhancements
Driver Centric Driving
•Trigonometry-based TeleOp control keeps
movement aligned with the driver’s view.
•Robot moves relative to the driver,
regardless of field orientation.
•Simplifies driving and reduces workload,
enhancing maneuverability.
•Improves precision and responsiveness
for efficient control.



State Machine
•State machine enables four-button TeleOp control for seamless sample handling.
•Automates pickup, deposit, and reset, simplifying operations.
•Supports single and dual-driver modes, reducing complexity.
•Enhances efficiency and minimizes driver workload.

How do we Iterate so quickly?: To iterate upon our designs, our team rigidly adheres to a system of learning from previous designs, game strategies, and
driver experience on the field. Once a robot has been completed, our team goals and strategies constantly iterated, which helps us iterate our robot
design. For example, the main design requirement that prompted the build of our Mark III robot was to optimize scoring points without sacrificing
defence, so we incorporated an active intake on the same level and a lighter Spindexer to sort the game pieces faster. To meet these design requirements,
our robot was 3D modeled using CAD software Onshape and was built in a matter of weeks using industrial design and fabrication processes. We
completed software using subsystem-based design in parallel with the mechanical design.

                       100% CAD Based Design
We used OnShape Software to CAD design our whole robot in the first few
weeks after kickoff. Using CAD allowed us to create custom parts using
                                                                                                            Our CNC Milling Capabilities
methods like 3D printing or CNC machining, giving much more design             Our team also uses CNC milling to fabricate 2D and 3D parts. Since our
flexibility than a solely kit-based approach.                                  robot is 100% CAD designed, CNC fabrication is easy to set up. This year,
                                                                               we have worked greatly to increase our team’s CNC Capabilities. We now
We organized our CAD design into subsystems and used joints to define
                                                                               have 3 working CNC mills, our custom-made MP (mostly printed) CNC,
how the parts would move. This allowed us to confirm that our design
                                                                               perfect for making large parts like side-plates, one small 6x6 industrial
could accomplish all of the planned objectives and make sure that the

moving parts had enough space. Using joints also allowed us to experiment
                                                                               finally our large-scale “PrintNC” CNC Mill.
with more complex features like linkages, which we used effectively.

                   .   \




              INNOVATing to push the envelope
                                                       Field-Centric Driving Trigonometry
• TeleOp driving is configured to the driver’s perspective, ensuring movement is
  independent of robot orientation.                                                     sidePower = tempForwardPower * Math.cos(angle) +
                                                                                        tempSidePower * Math.sin(angle);
• Trigonometry-based control converts robot coordinates to field coordinates,
  simplifying maneuverability.                                                          forwardPower = -tempForwardPower * Math.sin(angle)
• Uses gamepad inputs (tempForwardPower, tempSidePower) and gyroscopic                  + tempSidePower * Math.cos(angle);
  angle to compute movement.

                                                         PIDF Control & Motion Profiling
 • Custom PIDF controller provides flexibility beyond open-source solutions, ensuring precise motor control
   and stability.
 • Features include a customizable feedforward function based on position, velocity, and acceleration,
   enhancing responsiveness and efficiency.
 • Integral reset prevents windup, improving control accuracy and system reliability.

  on solid disk mass and radius
• Ball energy is taken from and equal to slow down in the rotating
  flywheel mass during launch
 Flywheels transfer ~ 7.5 kg m2 / second 2 of energy to the ball, loosing
 ~20% of their rotation speed on a launch, before motor begins to recover.

                                                                                                                                    2
What Every Team Member Should Know About Our Team
This is the baseline knowledge everyone should be able to explain clearly in judging.




Our Engineering Process


We follow the DHF Engineering Process: Define, Hypothesize, Fabricate.


     •    First, we break down the game and define what actually matters.
     •    Then we hypothesize using data and simulations.
     •    Finally, we fabricate, test, and iterate.

We don’t make decisions based on opinion. We use:


     •    Game Points Matrix (scoring value vs difficulty)
     •    Monte Carlo simulations (predict match outcomes)
     •    FMEA (risk and failure analysis)

If someone asks how we make decisions, the answer is:
We compare scoring value, simulate performance, analyze risk, then iterate.




Our Robot Evolution


We went from GB1 → GB2 → GB3.


     •    GB2 was heavier and focused more on defense.
     •    GB3 is lighter, faster, and optimized for scoring efficiency.

We redesigned because data showed GB2 limited acceleration and cycle speed.

One major lesson:
Simplicity and efficiency beat complexity.


We also mathematically proved that fast release was more efficient than sorting in autonomous.




Our Strategy


Using the Game Points Matrix, we prioritized:


     •    High-efficiency shooting cycles
     •    Reliable intake and spindexer indexing
     •    Strong autonomous scoring (far and close)
     •    Endgame park
     •    Ability to play defense when needed

We focused on maximizing scoring efficiency and consistency instead of trying to do everything.




                                                                                                  3
Our Control System & Software


Sensors we use:


     •    Encoders
     •    Optical odometry
     •    Limelight (AprilTags)
     •    HuskyLens
     •    Color sensors in the spindexer


Control systems:


     •    PIDF motor control
     •    Field-oriented drive
     •    State machine architecture
     •    Camera-based aiming
     •    Sensor fusion (odometry + vision)

We originally tried pure camera sorting, but it wasn’t consistent in real matches.
Switching to odometry + camera made it much more reliable.




How We Track Progress


We document everything through:


     •    Engineering Notebook
     •    Portfolio
     •    Posters
     •    Goal Tracker
     •    Sprint planning meetings
     •    FMEA documentation

We meet every two weeks for sprint planning and adjust based on data and performance.




Outreach – A Major Part of Our Identity


Our main outreach programs:


     •    Computer Outreach Project
     •    Rookie Qualifier
     •    Production Hub
     •    Veterans & Hospital Outreach
     •    Webinar series
     •    Maker Faire recruiting

We have donated:


     •    56 computers this season




                                                                                        4
     •     156 computers all-time

We’ve supported teams locally, nationally, and internationally (Libya, Jamaica, Vietnam, Arizona, Wisconsin, Milwaukee Robotics
Academy).


We don’t just compete — we expand access to STEM infrastructure.




Professional Relationships


We maintain active relationships with:


     •     PRSG sponsors
     •     Healthcare and recreation therapy professionals
     •     Manufacturing and IT professionals

We learn real-world engineering application and how STEM impacts communities.


They are not official permanent mentors, but we stay in contact and learn from them.




Team Structure & Sustainability


We use:


     •     Student mentorship pipeline
     •     Subsystem leads
     •     Sprint planning
     •     Goal Tracker

Biggest challenge:
Integrating 9 new members.

Biggest accomplishment:
Successfully integrating 9 new members while staying competitive.

We focus heavily on long-term sustainability.




Financial Stability


Funding sources:


     •     PRSG
     •     Wisconsin DPI Grant
     •     Sponsors
     •     Parent donations
     •     Matching funds

We diversify funding and reuse parts when possible.

Budget is managed by the coach with student leader input.




                                                                                                                                  5
What Makes Us Dicerent


We combine:


     •    Data-driven engineering (Monte Carlo, FMEA, Game Points Matrix)
     •    Large-scale, sustained outreach
     •    Mid-season redesign based on performance data
     •    156 computer donations
     •    Rookie Qualifier leadership
     •    Production Hub support

We are both competitive and community-driven.




Core Values


     •    Data over opinion
     •    Simplicity over complexity
     •    Sustainability over short-term wins
     •    Outreach is strategic, not optional
     •    Continuous improvement
     •    Collaboration over competition




If Any Member Gets Pulled for Judging, They Should Be Able to Explain:


     •    DHF process
     •    Game Points Matrix
     •    Monte Carlo simulations
     •    FMEA
     •    Why we redesigned to GB3
     •    Why we switched from pure camera to sensor fusion
     •    What the spindexer does
     •    How many computers we donated
     •    How sprint planning works
     •    Our biggest lesson: simplicity and efficiency win

                  Intake System

                  horizontally. This increases speed, efficiency, and

NEW MEMBER PROCESS
Your path onto the team
             Express Interest
     1       Talk to us, attend a meeting and a panel interview, see if the vibe fits


             Join as Student Mentor
     2       If accepted, start as a mentor — paired with an experienced member and coach


             Pick Your Subteam
     3       Choose mechanical, software, outreach, or strategy based on your interests


             Work on a Project
     4       Get assigned a real, defined project within your subteam


             Grow & Transition
     5       As skills develop and roster allows, move to full Team Member status




 FTC GEARHEADS #16460                                                                       @ftcgearheads16460
EXPECTATIONS
What we ask of every team member

   Make Gearheads a Priority
   This is a commitment-level extracurricular. We provide flexibility, but attendance and follow-through matter.


   Define Your Contribution
   Every member sets goals and owns deliverables. You shape your role.


   Lead with FIRST Values
   Gracious professionalism, respect, and collaboration in everything you do.


   Step Up, Don't Just Show Up
   This is a student-led organization. Take initiative and be a leader.




 FTC GEARHEADS #16460                                                                                              @ftcgearheads16460
NEW MEMBER PROCESS
Path to the GEarheads

   It Is Not Just the Robots
   All new members join as a Student Mentor. Participate in robotics sub team, as well as being required to commit to Outreach activities.


   Student Mentor – or More Appropriately “Mentee”
   Registered with FIRST as a Student Mentor. Forms and introduction to the team with special project assignments and coaching to build
   skills.


   As a Mentor
   You join all team activities, obligations, and competitions. You typically would not join the rostered team during formal j udging
   presentations.


   Transitioning to Rostered Team Member
   Typically happens within 1 year of joining the team as long as you meet your obligations, grow, and you feel it is a good fit .




 FTC GEARHEADS #16460                                                                                                                   @ftcgearheads16460
PARENTS
Parent Support and Engagement are Critical

   School Activity Funding
   Team funding is 95% through donations, local company and DPI grants. Each rostered student at BCHS pays a club activity fee . Was
   $100 in 2025.

   Support Your Student
   We ask parents to provide travel costs/arrangements for their student and donate food/snacks to support team meetings. We ask that
   you support applications to your employer's STEM/community grant program if they have one. No other financial commitment.

   Volunteer
   Parents are asked to volunteer to help support special events and projects. Can be organizational tasks, volunteer to judge , volunteer to
   work concessions, car pool, etc.


   Coaching and Mentoring
   All coaches are parent volunteers. We typically have 4 to 5 parents in coaching roles – from engineering skills, to business, to
   communications.

## Season 2025-26 — DECODE

### FTC Gearheads Judging Script.docx

- **Original filename:** `FTC Gearheads Judging Script.docx`
- **File size:** 12 KB
- **Document type:** Word document — judging presentation script
- **Season/Year:** 2025-26 (DECODE)
- **Category:** Judging Materials
- **Team stated as '9th year as a FIRST team, 7th year in FTC'; notes all 10 founding members graduated the prior year**
- **Structured as a numbered speaking script with named speaker assignments (John, Shloka, Shivansh, Rhea, etc.)**

**Complete extracted content (verbatim):**

```text
[core_properties] title='Word Document' author='' created=None modified=datetime.datetime(2026, 7, 2, 19, 51, 37, tzinfo=datetime.timezone.utc) last_modified_by='python-docx'

[Heading 2] 1. Introduction - John
[normal] Hello judges, we would like to start with a 5-minute presentation. Would that be all right?

[normal] We are team #16460, the GEarheads. This is our 9th year as a FIRST team, and our 7th year in FTC. Our team consists of members and mentors from grades 6-12. This season has been very different for us, as all 10 of our founding members graduated last year.

[normal] 2. Goals – Shloka 
[normal] This season is about all of us stepping up to prove we can carry on. With this in mind, we set three goals as new leaders:
[normal] Grow technical and outreach skills across the team, keeping everyone engaged and learning
[normal] Expand our core outreach projects, aimed at bringing STEM to underserved communities and new FIRST groups.
[normal] Build a robot that combines strong engineering with competitive performance.

[Heading 2] 3.  Sustainability – Shivansh
[Heading 3] We realized sustainability had to be intentional.  So we formalized our subsystem documentation, created shadowing roles for new members, and organized our season into 2-week sprints with clear responsibilities, helping our structure stay strong through the transition.

[normal] We brought on two new coaches bringing fresh software and mechanical skills, continued a core group of previous coaches and mentors, and had support from our graduated alumni.

[Heading 2] 4. Hospital Outreach – Rhea
[normal] Focusing on community outreach, one of the most meaningful connections we have built is with the Children's Hospital of Wisconsin. We brought STEM-based play to kids undergoing long-term hospitalization and treatment.
[normal] We worked with child life specialists to shape this into a bi-monthly program, bringing activities like paper circuits, catapults, and small pneumatic machines that patients can build live from their rooms or do in person.
[normal] This season, we ran four hybrid workshops for 40+ patients. One patient barely spoke in the first minutes of a session, but by the end, she was confident, cracking jokes and showing us her catapult that she completed before we finished the instructions. 
[normal] Engineering isn’t just technical —it can grow confidence.

[Heading 2] 5. Veterans Outreach  - Livia

[normal] Growing from the Hospital program, we found another group at the VA Spinal Cord Injury Unit. We worked with the professiona staff at the VA to execute two six-week robotics FLL boot camps. Veterans learned programming and robot design, and we ended with a friendly competition.

[normal] This season, we’ve invested about 130 hours across 12 weekly VA sessions. Robotics became a way to build skills and community.

[Heading 2] 6. Supporting and Advocating for FIRST – Abhay 
[normal] We are also committed to demonstrating the impact of FIRST in our broader community. We demonstrated CNC machining at the 2 day Milwaukee Maker Faire event, reaching hundreds of families. We have reached out with robot demos at Elmbrook Education Foundation STEM fairs,  Elmgrove Library demos, and FLL exhibition events.
[normal] To grow that community we have also stepped up our Advocacy for FIRST in Wisconsin. 
[Heading 2] Continued - Aryav
[normal] With a strong team member placed on the Wisconsin FTC Student Advocacy Board we have been able to help build tools to understand the needs of new FIRST teams and build plans to get them those resources.
[normal] Supporting  Government Advocacy Day in Madison, we shared our personal stories with three legislators, and advocated for expansion of the Wisconsin Public Instruction grant.
[normal] We represented Wisconsin at the first ever FTC Governors Cup event in Washington DC.  We spent over an hour with Governor Evers, his wife, and two policy staff members sharing the impact of FIRST, our own stories, our challenges, and discussing growing FIRST in Wisconsin.

[Heading 2] 7. Computer/STEM Access – Dirk
[normal] We also make an impact by providing technical opportunities and FIRST access for teams.. With help from Wisconsin FIRST, Milwaukee Robotics Academy, and our supporting donors, we’ve worked to close the digital divide by collecting, refurbishing, and securely reimaging donated computers. 
[normal] This year we have delivered 54 laptops, with a total of 150 donated over the course of five years. We have impacted over 500 students this year and several teams we’ve supported have had very successful seasons, showing a lasting impact.

[Heading 2] 8. Rookie Qualifier – Anirudh
[Heading 3] Strengthening FIRST in Wisconsin, we ran our second Rookie Qualifier  “Skirmish in Brookfield”, hosting 26 rookie teams.    To support their learning, we ran a five-week webinar series giving them tools to have a successful qualifier experience.

[normal] The qualifier brought in 3 new FTC judge volunteers, provided extra technical and language support, and created an environment for new teams to learn.
[Heading 3] Beyond the rookies - our team produced 41 CNC machined parts for teams as part of the FIRST production hub, and provided fabrication coaching to four FTC teams. 
[Heading 3] We don’t just compete in FIRST — we try to leave it stronger than we found it.

[Heading 2] 9. Design Process – Roman
[normal] We’ve continued to build strong relationships with engineers from companies like Milwaukee Tool, Milliman, GE Healthcare, Generac,  and Johnson Controls.   These experts help us learn professional engineering practices
[normal] We have modeled our design  process after GE Healthcare’s Design History Framework. We use a Game Points Matrix to guide and prioritize strategy, FMEA to anticipate failure, and even built a Monte Carlo simulation to estimate scoring performance before competition. 

[normal] 10. Design Iterations – John
[normal] We built three robots this season.  It has stretched our team but allowed us to grow and learn:
[normal] Our first design at the 30-hour-build used a claw intake. It worked — but under defense, our cycle time was inconsistent..
[normal] We redesigned our 2nd iteration with an active intake and spindexer system, reducing jams and making performance more repeatable.  It had good capability,  but lacked speed.
[normal] Our third iteration is designed to be fast and consistent:
[normal] A new automatically aimed turret increases speed and launching accuracy
[normal] An improved spindexer, feeding 3 balls in 900 milliseconds
[normal] A self limiting intake eliminates jamming and excessive ball intake
[normal] We learn with every iteration.
[Heading 2] 12. Software – Victor 
[normal] On software, we ramped up three core members with a new coach and focused on:  
[normal] Utilizing Optical odometry with Pedro pathing to increase localization speed and accuracy
[normal] Deploying a position hold algorithm that actively maintains the robot position during launching when pushed by another robot.
[normal] Implementing a motion profile based PIDF controller to rotate the turret smoothly and responsively through a custom camera and odometry localization fusion system.

[normal] 13. Closing – Manav 
[normal] To us, the GEarheads represent a team that grows technically, strengthens its community, and builds programs that last. Even after 10 founding members graduated, our systems held strong.
[normal] Thank you. We’d love to take any questions.
```

---

[Heading 3] GEarheads Judging Presentation
[normal] Revised March 5, 2025


[normal] 1. Introduction - Sid 

[normal] Hello judges, we would like to start with a 5-minute presentation, would that be all right?

[normal] We are team #16460, the GEarheads. This is our 8th year as a FIRST team, and our 6th year in FTC. We’d like to share our goals and accomplishments from this season with you.


[normal] 2. Goals - Sean

[Heading 3] As a team, our main goals this season were:

[normal] To strengthen our community connections building a broader FIRST and STEM awareness across Wisconsin
[normal] To drive outreach projects, focusing on making new FTC teams successful and introducing them to new skills.
[normal] To emphasize mentoring and engaging younger student mentors to improve our team's sustainability
[normal] And, as always, to combine technology and a strong engineering process to create a fun competitive robot.


[normal] 3. Hospital Outreach Highlights - Rhea 

[normal] One of the most meaningful connections we have built is with Children's Hospital of Wisconsin. We learned about “play therapy” to bring STEM-based play to kids undergoing long-term hospitalization and treatment, using STEM and FIRST.

[normal] We worked with numerous experts including Dr. Jeffrey Karst and Leslie Scott at Children's Hospital to refine this into a bi-monthly program, bringing projects like paper circuits, catapults, and pneumatic machines!

[normal] This season, we have held 5 in-person and live-streamed STEM workshops at Children’s for 50+ patients. We love to see the impact of engaged patients and smiling faces.

[normal] We expanded this project and have started new partnerships with Sean Clark at the Milwaukee War Memorial and Veterans Association, delivering our first in-person session to mentor veterans at the VA Hospital.

[normal] 4. Computer Outreach/STEM Access - Josef 

[normal] We are proud of the growth of our computer project, which, over the past four years, has donated 109 computers to STEM students and Robotics teams that lack the resources.  

[normal] This season, we donated 49 laptops and brought 10 laptops to give out during this competition.


[normal] 5. Connecting with STEM Experts - Misha 

[normal] As a team, we have connected with over 35 new STEM leaders. We focused on connecting and sharing their expertise with others in the FIRST community.

[normal] Our connections include engineers from Milwaukee Tool, Milliman, GE Healthcare, and Johnson Controls. Other experts helped us spread FIRST in the community through the Milwaukee Maker Faire or 
[normal] at TWIST. 

[normal] These connections helped us develop as a team, improve our skills with industrial insight, and grow FIRST in the community. Additionally, having 2 members on the Student Board of Directors helped us to grow the Wisconsin Production Hub project and to advocate for FIRST to companies like Johnson Controls.


[normal] 6. Connecting with Government - Sid

[normal] We know there are gaps in local support and we are focusing on creating positive change through our school district and state government. This includes:

[normal] Working with current state representative Adam Neylon and the WI advocacy committee to pass a new $4.5 million robotics grant in the WI state budget.
[normal] We worked with our school district to get another robotics-dedicated space and to find money to register all 22 FTC and FLL teams and pay for fields.
[normal] STEM Chips…


[normal] 7. Supporting FIRST - Avaneesh 

[normal] By using Sprint Planning, giving us the ability to bring our team in front of our community to demonstrate FIRST values. Our 9 new student mentors this year have been a strong part of this, taking on projects focused on spreading FIRST understanding. This includes:

[normal] Demonstrating our CNC capability at 2 Milwaukee area Maker Fair Events - touching hundreds of community members
[normal] Building 2 Little Libraries for the March of Dimes organization, Raising $5,200 for the organization
[normal] Driving our CNC project, coaching new teams to build CNC machines and learning how to use them

[normal] We are also very proud to have hosted the “Skirmish in Brookfield” Rookie Qualifier, hosting 19 new teams and nearly 300 students. This event helped make qualifiers more accessible for rookie teams by:

[normal] Providing a 5-week webinar series giving them tools to be ready for the event
[normal] Supplying extra FTAs and a dedicated technical support booth



[normal] 8. Design Process - Eduardo 

[normal] Let’s introduce our robot. The core of our design process is the DHF (Design History Framework) Design Process from GE.

[normal] We start by considering robot requirements with a Game Points Matrix and identifying scoring capabilities and their difficulty. This design tradeoff tool feeds an FMEA error analysis system in conjunction with a custom-made Monte Carlo simulation to help us ensure reliable and predictable performance. Using CAD, we can then develop and test subsystems for each of these functions.


[normal] 9. Design Iterations - Matthew 

[normal] Our iterative design process helps us create the ideal robot that meets the goals we set. We have built 3 robot revisions this season, starting with our 30-hour build robot, which was shared with over 30,000 viewers. Since then, our current robot has an OPR ranked 14th in the World, and we were recognized by the FUN FTC network as the 9th best robot in March.

[normal] 10. Robot Design - Thomas 

[normal] Using the DHF, we make three critical design choices for our robot.
[normal] For a quick and stable robot, we placed all motors at the base of the robot along the drivetrain to lower the robot’s center of mass.
[normal] We wanted a scoring system that could deliver both samples and specimens, so we incorporated a complex differential lift and differential claw, yielding 6 degrees of freedom.
[normal] For a compact robot, also designed a transmission system that delivers power from the drivetrain to the ascent mechanism during endgame.


[normal] 11. Mechanical Design and Fabrication - Matthew 

[normal] We used many processes to fabricate our fully CADed design, such as extensive CNC machining on our custom-built mills. Our tools and processes helped us build the first iteration of this robot in under a month.

[normal] 12. Software Design Process - Victor

[normal] The FOVs for both drivers transfer the robot’s frame of reference to the driver's. 

[normal] We implemented optical odometry which offloads calculation on-chip, increasing both the speed and accuracy of localization.

[normal] PID Plus Motion Profiling controls our Differential Elevator Drive. We are able to have two motors control the lift rotational and translational movement at the same time.  

[normal] Lastly, we are leveraging our vision-based control algorithm at this event. The robot is able to autonomously locate blocks in the submersible and place them in the bucket.


[normal] 13. Living FIRST Values and Investing in FIRST - Thomas

[normal] Thank you, and we would now be glad to take questions.



[normal] All team members need to know the basics of the following Rubric Notes:

[normal] Be able to discuss the basic answers to each Rubric Element.
[normal] Be able to give examples of some details that you worked on yourself or you are aware of.
[normal] Be able to point to further resources in the Pit that show the rubric element in more detail.

[normal] Innovate (John, Yuva)
[normal] Describe engineering content.  To show how the team arrived at an engineering design solution.
[normal] Used Sprint planning to divide work into subteams
[normal] Prototyped multiple design iterations of subsystems
[normal] Robot or mechanism is creative and unique
[normal] We use 2 differential drives, which allow us to keep the center of mass as low as possible, making our robot more stable
[normal] We use a PTO system which utilizes the same motors that drive the robot to lift it during endgame, cutting out the need for additional motors.
[normal] Our FOV system simplifies driving by allowing our drive team to move the robot relative to their point of view rather than the robot’s.
[normal] Creative designs come with additional risk - document how you remediate these risks
[normal] We use FMEA (Failure Mode Effect Analysis) to analyze the potential ways our robot could fail and find ways to prevent them.

[normal] Motivate (Tanvi, Victor)
[normal] Describe team organization goals,finances and sustainability plan, risk management planning, season timeline, outreach and service plan
[normal] Team organization: Student mentor program, 
[normal] Outreach: hospital outreach, computer outreach, community connections
[normal] Management planning: sprint plans, sub team meetings
[normal] Finances: strategic investments, and financial tracking, funding sources
[normal] Individual contributions of each team member, and how they apply to the success of the team.
[normal] Individual student goals 
[normal] Ambassador to FIRST programs and recruiting people not in the FIRST program
[normal] Twist event, Maker Faires, Production Hub, state advocacy, local school district robotics effort
[normal] Show lessons learned from that outreach
[normal] FMEA - GE healthcare; 
[normal] Creative approach to marketing FIRST


[normal] Control  (Shawn, Manav, Eduardo)
[normal] HW/SW components and challenges each solves
[normal] Hardware: Claw, PTO, Elevator
[normal] Software: Odometry, Video Based camera detection, Dual FOVs
[normal] Look at software and mechanical innovations poster in pit.
[normal] HW/SW solutions to improve functions using feedback and control
[normal] Use of FMEA and Monte-Carlo simulation to find failure modes and inefficient systems, then figure out how to mitigate those failure modes. 
[normal] For example changing previous claw to a bigger claw to reduce failure of pickups.
[normal] Show how solutions are reliable and effective
[normal] Took lots of data and compared with previous robot iterations in the monte carlo simulation to find the most reliable and effective solution.
[normal] Look at the robot iterations poster in the pit & ask Ed.
[normal] Use of engineering process to develop control solutions (Sensors, HW, algorithms)  
[normal] Designed PTO (modeled at back of pit), Differential drive (modeled at back of pit), Autonomous in Tele Op (effect showed on control poster)

[normal] Design Award (Dante, Roman)
[normal] Describe how robot is elegant, efficient, and practical 
[normal] Efficient with motors lowering the center of mass with less wobble and the claw having a video based control algorithm to easily locate blocks.
[normal] Elegant with claw having a simple grabbing motion which is still really fast and reliable and robot does the job even with a small design.
[normal] Entire machine design is worthy of the recognition 
[normal] Packed design ensures the robot won’t fall without sacrificing looks. 3D printed nameplates slide into place above the custom made side plates. The PTO makes sure to not sacrifice space with more motors implementing the drivetrain motors into our Level 3 hang. The differential belt allows the motors controlling the height and rotation of the arm to be down. 
[normal] Distinguishes itself by aesthetic and functional design
[normal] Basis for design is well considered
[normal] Added subsystems and made it very simple, instead of having a complex process to transfer sample to bucket, we use just one claw and modify it to be fast and have other improvements. 
[normal] Design is effective and consistent with the game plan/strategy
[normal] Incorporating the claw with a differential lift and 6 degrees of freedom makes it consistent in scoring basket and chamber.
[normal] All motors placed around the drivetrain keep the robot from wobbling back and forth, making the robot fast and smooth with almost no concerns.

[normal] Design Process
[normal] Focus on game point matrix, subsystem prototypes, leads to optimization of robot.  New materials, FMEA analysis leads to verification testing and reliability with extensive scrimmage plans. [normal] 	Hardware
[normal] Foundation of robot drive 7 motors, with two differential drives - allowing for the control of multiple axis motion from one motor set.
[normal] New technologies this year - fabrication of carbon fiber parts for weight reduction, transmission PTO for lift.  Use of linear bearing for a compact and stiff lift mechanisms with a string drive to allow rotation around 4 of our 6 degrees of freedom on the arm.

[normal] 	Software and Control
[normal] Dual POV for both robot driver position and Claw Pick up position
[normal] Dual nested PID loops for control of multiple axis simultaneously
[normal] Integrated laser odometry with one of the first integrations of that technology into Road Runner
[normal] Automated Video location of samples in the submersible
[normal] Control simplification that integrates autonomous sub tasks into teleop - for example a wall to specimen median hang time of 4.52 seconds.

[Heading 2] Team History
Team 16460 Gearheads is a 6th year FIRST team, and a 4th year FTC Team. Our team has gone to the Worlds competition twice; one time for FLL in Detroit in 2018 and the other time in 2022 to Houston.  Our team is based out of the Elmbrook School District, and calls Brookfield Central High School home. Our team ranges from 8th graders to 10th graders, all in the same school district. Our team has 12 full time team members, some junior team members, and 4 head coaches.
Over the previous 5 seasons our team has been extremely blessed with the opportunities that we have been able to have, and we always try to make the most out of these. We have won numerous awards, from the Champions award in FLL to the Inspire award in FTC, as well as winning multiple FTC robot games at qualifiers and once at the state championships. 
First FLL Season – 2017-2018 
[IMAGE #2: embedded image in paragraph]
The GEarheads started as an FLL team in 2017, we consisted of 10 5th grade students from Swanson Elementary, our team number was 31191. We still have 5 members from then that are still with us today - Josef, Mathew, Kartik, Misha, and Josh. That season we made it to sectionals. This season’s theme was hydrodynamics and our project was to create a composting machine that would motivate people to compost in the same way that Fitbit motivates people to take steps and this compost would motivate people to compost. This would cause composting to take over fertilizer helping water quality. 
Second FLL Season – 2018-2019
In our second year many of the members of our team changed, Sid is the only one who joined and is still on our team today. That year we had a lot of success. We got first at state advancing us to the world festival and we earned first place for the Research Award. This season’s theme was into orbit our team’s project talked about how debris is becoming an exponentially bigger and bigger problem so we designed solar panels that would be able to retract into the spaceship to avoid debris and be easily repaired.
[IMAGE #3: embedded image in paragraph]

First FTC Season – 2019 – 2020
[IMAGE #4: embedded image in paragraph]
After two years of FLL, we decided to compete in FTC as a team. We were one of three FTC teams in our school district. Many of the people that joined our team that year we had seen on different teams at FLL competitions. This was the first year we had members of different ages, our team consisted of 7th-11th graders. We had a great season making our way to the finals at state. This season’s theme was Skystone, for this season we designed a robot that is agile and could position itself very easily. We had trouble using Vuforia and stacking more than 4 blocks consistently.

Second FTC Season – 2020 – 2021
[IMAGE #5: embedded image in paragraph]
During our second year of FTC, our members didn’t change that much. We lost some older members to the beast robotics FRC team and others quit because of Covid. We got picked by the first alliance, we had a lot of success in the winning alliance at the state competition. Our strategy was focused on getting as many points as possible in autonomous by preparing for many possibilities with Vuforia. While our teleop strategy was based on getting as many rings into the goals as possible.
Third FTC Season – 2021 – 2022
[IMAGE #6: embedded image in paragraph]
During our third year of FTC, we gained three new members, Thomas, Vaidehi, and Yuva. This year we had a new team organizational structure, we were focused on being well-rounded, earning us our first Inspire award at the first qualifier we attended. Then at state, we were the winning alliance captain qualifying us for our first World competition as an FTC team, in Houston, Texas.


[Heading 2] Team Plans and Goals
Team Mission Statement 
The mission of FIRST Team 16460, the GEarheads, is to inspire and engage students in the Wisconsin to participate in Science Technology and Engineering and Math and FIRST Robotics. We look to inspire students by giving them opportunities to participate on FIRST Robotics teams that allow them to discover new skills and ideas, persist through problems and solve them with innovative solutions, have the sense of being included in a meaningful team trying to achieve an important goal, promoting the idea of teamwork and group work, and finally, helping them make an impact on their team, FIRST, STEM and the world as a whole. 

GEarheads, as a team, has been maturing.  In all four years as an FTC team, we qualified for the Wisconsin State Championship, and last year we were proud to represent Wisconsin at the FIRST Worlds in Houston.

This season our main goals are:
Improve team effectiveness and efficiency by assigning clear roles, tracking progress, and increasing accountability
Ensure team sustainability by adding 3 new junior members and expanding FLL mentoring and outreach
Upskill tech capabilities in design, custom fabrication, and automation software
Grow community outreach and STEM connections for greater impact

We are especially proud of our major outreach efforts that 
Improved technology access to under-resourced robotics teams in the greater Milwaukee Area, which also lead to helping a school in Honduras
Provided play therapy through STEM Workshops for patients and childhood illness survivors by working with Children’s Hospital of Wisconsin, and 
Taught CAD skills workshops to FLL teams, who will eventually move to FTC or FRC teams.


Improve Team Effectiveness and Efficiency 

[IMAGE #7: embedded image in paragraph]
In this third season for FTC, we really wanted to refine our team composition and sustainability. The biggest change we brought this year was that we defined a more formal team charter & roles; also we had everyone apply for those roles based on their interest, expertise, and willingness to learn. This was followed by interviews with coaches.  
This year we made sure everyone had a role they enjoyed, and that everyone completed the work they promised to complete. We did this using a Sprint Plans, which helped set deadlines for the work we had to complete, and if someone didn’t follow through with what they had promised to complete twice, they would be removed from the team. We also used a Gantt Chart to organize the tasks that had to be completed. 

Sustainability Plan

 Our sustainability plan is focused in four main areas.
Actively recruit new team members via FIRST/STEM Expos. This year we added three new junior members, Shashank, Victor, and John, and one team member, Rhea.  These new members have been improving the team in terms of Software, Mechanical, and have had a major role in outreach projects, such as CAD Outreach and Children’s Hospital STEM workshops

Investing time with FLL teams by mentoring them weekly, volunteering at events as well as scrimmages. We drove focused efforts on mentoring three FLL teams all season. We mentored 3 FLL teams this year, Team B.U.I.L.D. (44199), team Stark Energy (57921), and FLL Lightning Bolts (53650). All teams qualified for the Sectionals or the State Championship and team BUILD is moving onto Worlds. We mentored these teams in CAD design, their project, and their robot design.

We were very involved with Stark Energy, a rookie FLL team, with helping them up to multiple times a week with project and robot work.  We also helped teams such as the FLL Team B.U.I.L.D. from printing 3D parts for them to giving them input on their project survey and giving advice and help for their Worlds preparation. Our goal is to inspire them to go into FTC or FRC once they enter their late middle school and early high school years through our mentoring and volunteering efforts.

Hosting Introductory CAD Sessions for FLL teams. CAD or Computer Aided Design has been a core part of our Design Process all through our time as an FTC team. We recognized that many FLL teams would need this skill in CAD as they progressed into being a FTC team. 
We developed a course syllabus that would let kids new to CAD easily adapt and learn the software. We have had two sessions with a great turnout (17 kids across 2 FLL teams), and we are planning more sessions for the future. The kids from the FLL teams we are hosting these sessions for, will eventually feed into our team after they are done with FLL, and when they do they will have valuable skills such as CAD.

[IMAGE #8: embedded image in paragraph]
Through our EEF events such as the Circle of Friends and Explore STEM events we raised awareness in our community about FIRST and STEM. We talk to people about First and STEM, and give anyone interested an opportunity to drive our robot, to give them a hands on experience. This gives anyone interested an opportunity to join our team. We have had over 250+ people attending the event.

Growing Community Connections and STEM Connections

Connected with 1000+ community members through events and media exposure…
[IMAGE #9: embedded image in paragraph]

- Between last season and this season we collected 124 computers/devices at 11 events across our community, and delivered 16 to under-resourced FIRST Teams/STEM Organizations in MKE and 5 to kids in Honduras.
- Designed and led 2 Children’s Hospital STEM Workshops for patients, and invited to do them every 2 mo. through Dec. 2023
- Co-hosted booth and connected with guests at Maker Faire Milwaukee with Northwestern Mutual iTech Team
- Demonstrated at EEF Circle of Friends (250+ attendees, May ‘22) 
- Designed and Delivered 2 CAD Workshops attended by 2 FLL Teams.
- Weekly mentoring of FLL Stark Energy
- Volunteered at FLL Expos, Scrimmages, and Regional Qualifiers.
- Volunteered at FTC qualifiers, including the Battle of Brookfield qualifier
- Engaged kids at EEF Explore STEAM Event (300+ kids plus parents attending)(Mar. 7)
- Successfully advocated for a dedicated FTC Robotics room at school

New Significant Community Connections:
Ashish Udani, Senior Application Engineer · Midwest Engineered Systems Inc.: We organized a meeting with Mr. Udani where we learned about Career Applications of Robotics and Automation. We are also looking forward to having a tour of his company soon in the future. 
Vijay Sankaran - Vice President and CTO of Johnson Controls:  We were very fortunate this year to have the chance to host the CTO of Johnson Controls, Mr. Vijay Sankaran. We hosted a zoom webinar and invited all Wisconsin FTC and FRC to join. Due to the theme of FIRST Energize, we got the chance to learn about Johnson Control’s work in Energy Sustainability. Mr. Sankaran also taught us the methods and processes of Disruptive Innovation. We are incredibly fortunate to host such a prolific and impactful speaker.
Leslie Scott - Certified Child Life Specialist - Children's Wisconsin: We met with Mrs. Scott to begin our Flagship Outreach project. She gave us the information and advice necessary to communicate with the Patients at the Hospital.
Dr. Jeffery Karst- PhD is a specialist in Hematology/Oncology/BMT, Pediatric & Psychology:  His research in play therapy helped to formalize our strategy in developing our presentation and sessions.
Daniel Medeiros - Chief Engineer, and Sr. Manager: Mr. Medeiros was our Key Connection to Milwaukee Tool. Inc. Mr. Medeiros gave us a presentation on the company’s design method that allows them to make and design efficient products at an incredible speed.  We got to experience a guided tour of the Milwaukee Tools Facility. We learned about the facility and their methods as they design and test new products. This was a very informative session where we got to see a Design Process in action in the industrial World.
Chad Lawson- Maker Space Ship:  Chad is a childhood survivor who uses STEM to inspire other survivors.  He went through a workshop with us about how he creates these STEM workshops for childhood survivors
Jimmie Beacham- Executive Chief Engineer at GE - Advanced Manufacturing:  He gave us a tour at his facility and showed us how GE is accelerating it’s business with additive manufacturing.
Upskill Tech Capabilities
We wanted to upskill both our software and mechanical tech capabilities, and we have through our robot as we have build the most sophisticated robot of all our 4 FTC seasons. 99% CAD based design, 90% custom-made parts. Sophisticated programming with Roadrunner, OpenCV & Dead Wheel Odometry & sophisticated control mechanisms. We also used subsystem based design and Integration to achieve an efficient design process

Design Process:
At the beginning of our Season, our Game Strategy Team worked with the Mechanical Team in order to highlight the goals for our Robot. We used the game manual and the FTC game release to highlight and rank the missions with the best payoff. Then, we discussed what subsystems and components would be necessary for accomplishing these important tasks. We created what we call a Game Matrix, and Robot Requirement Diagram to accomplish these tasks. You can see in this slide how our Goals that we made translated to our Robot Design. 

Drivetrain:
 This year’s game focuses on quick, and accurate navigation in the field. So, we needed a small drivetrain, with possibilities of navigation tools like Odometry pods for accurate driving in Autonomous. 

Intake:
Our first version was an active intake system with a sweeper mechanism that can intake the cones into the robot quickly and accurately
Our new version can grab the cones, reorient, and elevate to the front quickly as the robot moves.

Outtake:
We needed an Elevator design that would be able to reach all 4 levels of junctions quickly and accurately. This would mean we want the elevator to be able to score on the Ground Junction without the need for another subsystem.
We implemented a State machine with buttons that control the height of the elevator with only 2 buttons. Using our Wrist/Elbow design on the elevator, we can also score in the Ground Junction. 

Cone Scoring System:
We needed the Cone Scoring system to reliably grab the cone and quickly score it on the Junction. 

Odometry:
This year, we used dead-wheel odometry is a method of tracking the robot’s position during Autonomous. We use 3 omni wheels connected to rotary encoders. 

In terms of software some improvements we had were:

This year we improved our Design process based on our learnings from last three seasons. The key highlights are

We developed a TestJig with representative final robot hardware and built the basic subsystem hardware in order to begin coding.
Using the developed drive-train code from the TestJig, we were able to quickly integrate Tele-Op and Autonomous, and start planning strategy early while testing.
Our Software and Hardware was Subsystem based design which allowed us to design, test, optimize and then integrate each of the 4 robot subsystem which resulted in huge improvement in schedule & reliability
Key learnings applied from GE Healthcare’s Design History File process by using Subsystem and API based design, Failure Mode analysis, Requirement trace down & UML based documentation


[Heading 1] Game Strategy
[Heading 2] Game Strategy Process:

Using the Design History Framework (DHF) by General Electric (GE) which we were able to learn about and implement into our subsystem based designs. The 10 step history framework allows us to be methodical and build the most suitable robot for each year’s different game. The first part of the DHF is solely our game strategy. To initiate the process we start by using a game point matrix for both Teleop and Autonomous, using this game point matrix we rank each task at a certain difficulty level and also the amount of points each of those tasks can get us. Using that we then, with the mechanical team, discuss what types of robot actions we will need to complete in order to continue by the game strategy we selected. 

Below shows our game point matrix, what robot capabilities we wanted at the beginning of the season on mechanical and software.

Autonomous Paths (Both Left and Right)
[IMAGE #10: embedded image in paragraph]
[Heading 1] Mechanical 

[Heading 2] Robot Subsystems 


Whole Robot View (Mark II)		
[IMAGE #11: embedded image in paragraph]




















For our Mark II robot, we wanted to improve upon all previous designs, while still designing our robot mainly through subsystem-based process. Since one of our main deign goals was to reduce complexity and focus more on creating simpler parts, the Mark II has a reduced weight and fewer subsystems. However, when combined, the function and power of these subsystems far surpasses the function Mark I Robot design due to increased focus given to each of them and decreased mechanical complexity. The main subsystems of the Mark II are the drivetrain (with odometry pods), elevator, and claw; with all of these subsystems only requiring six motors and one servo to be powered (four motors for the drivetrain and two motors are for the elevator/claw rotation/guides, and one servo for the claw). The Mark II robot’s industrial-like power efficiency and speed is what we are the most proud of, and is what we feel will give us a competitive edge on the playing field.

The Drive Train with Odometery Pods

[IMAGE #12: embedded image in paragraph]

Just like our Mark I robot, the drivetrain is built to be compacted within the side plates of the robot with the motors protruding into the bottom; which is done using clever gearing and belting within the small ~2 inch space between the side plates (the odometry pods are also within the inner and outer side plates). This design allows for all other mechanical parts to be fit into the center of the robot, giving us the ability to pass cones through the center in a strong industrial design. In addition, the drivetrain is also around an inch and a half less wide than the previous Mark I design, further cutting weight and increasing maneuverability on the field. Finally, the inclusion of the odometry pods within the drivetrain grants them protection, and aid in the creation of a reliable 1+5 autonomous. The odometry pods gain their information from 3 encoders (one for each) that measure the rotation of the odometry wheels to determine the location and rotation of the robot on the field. All of this is possible using the power of four motors (one for each mecanum wheel) and three sensors (encoders).




The Elevator 
[IMAGE #13: embedded image in paragraph]
Unlike the Mark I, the elevator of the Mark II is completely vertical, and is much faster due to the reduced amount of contact between the slides and due to the decreased weight of its load (due to the industrial-like “Swiss Cheese Aluminum” cut by our very own MPCNC machine along with decreased complexity of the orienting mechanism/claw). After the claw grabs the cone, two motors are used to power the extension of the linear slides (strung together with a rubber belt), which allow the cone to reach any junction level (using a software programmed state machine). The motion of the elevator is stabilized with counterweights placed on the back of the robot and with a structural aluminum rod that connects to the lowest slide. The elevator, other than the slides and motors, is completely custom machined, as all of the rotating v-bearings and their fixtures are 3D printed. In total, the elevator requires two motors to power, which are also used to rotate the cone claw and power the cone guides at the same time, creating both linear and rotational motion in a unique and industrial design (will be explained further in the "claw grabber/orienting mechanism” section). 


The Claw Grabber and Orienting Mechanism 

[IMAGE #14: embedded image in paragraph]

Another large difference between Our Mark I and Mark II robots is the manner in which corners are picked up. For example, our Mark II robot uses a superior claw design that allows it to close further, gain a higher torque through gearing, and which includes a much more powerful and reliable servo to ensure reliability. In addition to this, maneuvering the claw has also been greatly improved. For example, the claw is much more easily lifted out of the robot (it is no longer enclosed), and its rotation is no longer powered with heavy and unreliable servos. Instead, a differential in angular velocity between the motors (which also power the elevator and cone guides) causes the claw mechanism to rotate, which greatly decreases weight, increases reliability, and increases the speed of the elevator. In addition to this, plexiglass guides that lift to envelop a pole junction have also been implemented onto the robot, which are also not powered with any servos. Instead, the power of the elevator (using the same two motors) and its position locks or unlocks the position of the guides, which are pulled into position by the rubber bands. Once the elevator moves to its starting position, the guides will retract and the cycle will repeat. Another feature we are proud of is the ability to detect the voltage being used the claw. From a large stream of data, our software team has figured out how to create a program that detects the voltage and strain of the servo. This feature is extremely useful for autonomous control, as if our robot misses a cone, it has conformation that it hasn’t picked one up and will aim for the same stack height again. In all, the new claw grabber and orienting mechanism has shown itself to be a reliable and efficient system that only requires the power of one servo and two motors, which are already powering two other actions, to operate, using an industrial design and clever programing.

Robot Design Iterations

Our First Iteration – 30 Hour Build
Our first robot was made during the 30-hour build, because of the time constraint we were not able to spend much time planning out the robot and optimizing it.

Pros | Cons
It got us 25,900 views on YouTube. It helped us decide what our next robots should be like Easy junction alignment Had a match 30 hours after the season started where we scored 94 points. Could grab cones reliably | Big (hard to navigate through the field) Very high center of gravity (it fell over, and tipped easily) Slow movement, and cycle time
[TABLE END]

Our first robot was made during the 30-hour build, because of the time constraint we were not able to spend much time planning out the robot and optimizing it.
[IMAGE #15: embedded image in paragraph]


Our Second Iteration - Mark I 


[IMAGE #16: embedded image in paragraph]


















[TABLE START]
Pros | Cons
Can pick up cones that fell. Extremely stable. Got a high score of 220. We had a lot of driver practice with this robot. Lower cycle time Durable/Built like a tank | Had to knock down cones to pick them up. (Sometimes severely hurt cycle times) Too heavy and bulky for navigating through field efficiently
[TABLE END]

The number of cones that fell during our 30 hour build matches prompted us to build an intake with the ability to pick up fallen cones. However, this added complexity, reduced cycle time, hurt navigation, which eventually caused us to build the Mark II.


Our Third (and Current) Iteration - Mark II

[TABLE START]
Pros | Cons
Much better cycle time. Light and fast Elevator Compact (Easy navigation, Speed) Much faster less complex intake Only one motor on top of the elevator  Greatly decreasing cycle time Removing the ability for the robot to fall over. Increases the speed of the elevator. Normal Claw Intake  Allows for a much smaller robot. Greatly decrease pickup cone time (old intake got jammed a lot, and had to knock down the cones first) | Less stable and durable as our last robot Cannot pickup fallen over cones
[TABLE END]

When we built our Mark I robot, we thought that a lot of the cones would be knocked over. So we built a robot that could pick up those cones. Later in the season, we found out that the cones don’t fall over very often, and there were many negative side effects to making it so our robot can pick up fallen-over cones. So, we made a robot that didn’t have these negative side effects, allowing it to perform much better.

Key Changes Made:
Our robot had two main changes between our first and second qualifiers. These included:
Replacing the mandrill with a regular claw.
Although the mandrill had loads of support, we found that it would be very difficult to ever grab cones from the stack without moving or knocking it over during autonomous. We thought that having an autonomous that had the ability to grab more than one cone was critical to do well in the current robot game.
The mandrill also strained the servo and was a constant point of failure due to the servo wearing out.
Getting rid of a complicated intake
Although the complicated intake could pick up cones that fell it had many drawbacks
Forced robot to be much bigger.
Speed
More difficulty navigating through junctions.
The intake often got jammed, which could cost us well over 20-30+ seconds by the end of a match. 
Attempts at improving the intake caused us many headaches, and wasn’t very successful

[Heading 2] Design Process and Framework
Engineering Design Process
As we went through our previous 3 years as an FTC team, we have learned the importance of having a defined and organized Engineering Design Process. Last year, in our 3rd year as an FTC team, we implemented the DHF (Design History Framework) format. This DHF design process is used by companies like GE Healthcare to design FDA approved machines, and this is where we learned about this process from. Our mentors at GE Healthcare taught us how to implement this process. The DHF process implements what is known as a subsystem based design, where every part of the final design is done individually, and tested before implementation. In this section of our Engineering Notebook, we will be walking through the phases of the design process. 

Phase 1 – Planning - based on the FTC game
This is the phase most heavily in play in the beginning of our FTC season. We use the FIRST FTC game manual along with the official game release to identify every possible way to score points (or mission) in this year’s game. After we have a list of each possible mission, our Game Strategy team identifies the pay off for each of these missions using a risk-reward analysis. 

Step 1 - The Game Points Matrix
The Game Points Matrix is the beginning of our DHF process. This Matrix presents a concise list of each mission’s payoff and worth in the game. This matrix is a very important tool where we start designing our Autonomous and Tele-op paths. But, this matrix isn’t the very first thing we start with. We start really basic, by identifying what we want our robot to do in Autonomous, Tele-op and Endgame, and what we need to have on the robot to accomplish that. After this has been identified and ranked by importance, we construct our Game Points Matrix. 
[IMAGE #17: embedded image in paragraph]
Step 2 - Design Autonomous and Tele-Op Paths
After the Game Points Matrix has been constructed, our Game Strategy team designed Autonomous and Tele-Op paths to start planning what subsystems we need on the robot to accomplish them. The Game Strategy team and the Software team worked together to create these diagrams. The Diagrams created spanned 3 difficulty levels that focussed on “high payoff” missions from the Game Matrix. 

Step 3 - User Requirements
During this stage we have to consider the technical requirements of the robot subsystems. This organization is especially important since later in the season many parts become unavailable. So it is important to discuss what we may need to make the robot. To achieve this mechanical and game strategy team collaborate so that we have a general idea of the technical specifications of the robot substems. This allows us to order the parts and design a basic outline of the robot subsystems in advance.

Steps 4-10 - Subsystem Requirements and Mechanical Fabrication
The next 6 steps require translating user requirements and game requirements to the robot, these steps of the Design History Framework will be covered in our fabrications section. 

[Heading 2] Fabrication and Manufacturing Techniques

[IMAGE #18: embedded image in paragraph]
Summary of Progress Thus Far:
This season, we have devoted ourselves to making a 100% CAD-designed robot, using a variety of Fabrication and Manufacturing techniques. These include 3D Printing and CNC machining, both of which allow for rapid prototyping and iterative design. This prototyping and iteration would lead us to design four robots in one season, in order to adapt to new challenges. These techniques in fabrication and manufacturing have helped us in creating a robust and well put-together robot capable of adapting to the situation at hand.


Computer Aided Design
By using CAD, we were able to create a much more fine-tuned robot, better suited to the requirements of the competition than something assembled using a kit. This is because rather than being constrained by the limitations of pre-existing parts, we can design our own parts to function just how we want them to. While more difficult, this technique of manufacturing and fabrication has greatly improved the overall quality of our robot.

[IMAGE #19: embedded image in paragraph]
Figure 1: Depicted above, it can be seen the bottom view of our robot’s drive train, as well as how the cone fits into our cone guide.
3D Printing
In conjunction with our use of CAD, we regularly use 3D Printing in order to fabricate some more complicated custom parts. Our robot uses many different 3D-printed parts in order to keep it working as intended, while still keeping the form factor small. 3D printing is also a great technique for prototyping parts and concepts before fully implementing them into our official robot design.
[IMAGE #20: embedded image in paragraph]
[IMAGE #21: embedded image in paragraph]


MPCNC
One of our main focuses this season in terms of manufacturing and fabrication processes was the construction of an MPCNC (Mostly Printed CNC), a Do-It-Yourself style CNC router built with 3D printed parts and common hardware components. In order to make larger parts more quickly and efficiently, we have worked to assemble this MPCNC using an open-source design. We have continued to use and iterate on this design, optimizing our manufacturing process.

Test Jig and Initial Code Design:

While the mechanical team was working hard on the robot the software team could not just wait and lose valuable time. Instead the team came up with the following two ideas (1) Develop a Test jig robot with all the hardware that the final robot was going to have (2) Write subsystem code to run the hardware & (3) Write calibration code to define the operational parameters for the hardware for e.g. the various limits for the servos, encoder value for the elevator & camera object detection using open CV.

Using the parameters given by the controller input we can move the robot.  This method decreases the power that would have normally been the input from the controller.
Differential Elevator | void setLiftElevatorLow() void setLiftElevatorMedium() void setLiftElevatorHigh()  void moveBucket() void bucketDown() void bucketUp() void bucketRest() | These methods set the position of the elevator system.  These methods set the position of the bucket that the cargo is placed in, and when executed in order tilt the cargo onto the shipping hubs.
