```text
[Heading 2] Incorporated Math and Designs

Motion Profiling + PIDF Control:

We need fast, reliable, and controlled lift and arm mechanism for our TeleOP and Autonomous state machines, where simple encoder-based designs are too imprecise.  We implemented our own PIDF (Proportional (P), Integral (I), Derivative (D), Feed-forward) control mechanism along with a custom motion profiling algorithm, to precisely control the lift and arm system. This system uses the error term e(t), and computes the power output needed to correct for this and translate to the next position in time.
[IMAGE #30: embedded image in paragraph]

[IMAGE #31: embedded image in paragraph]

We used a trapezoidal profile in v (velocity) vs. t (time), where the first phase has maximum acceleration, the second has maximum velocity, and the final phase has maximum deceleration. Taking the integral achieves the x (position) vs. t (time), of the elevator and rotational parameter of the arm, which is used by the robot PID controller to iteratively correct itself. The feed-forward aspect of the PIDF control is used to correct the gravitational force errors. (See theoretical vs. implementation graphs on robot controller)
[IMAGE #32: embedded image in paragraph]











[IMAGE #33: embedded image in paragraph]

Driver Centric Driving:

We used sophisticated trigonometry to implement our TeleOP driving which is configured to the point of view of the driver. With this the robot always moves relative to the driver’s view independent of the robot's orientation on the field which unburdens the driver and greatly simplifies the driving in TeleOp. We have been able to build three drive teams this year due to this simplification.

[IMAGE #34: embedded image in paragraph]

In the perspective of the driver, let x represent the forward power. Let y represent the side power. Finally let theta represent the angle the robot makes with the vertical. The inputs of the robot are parametrized as follows,
[IMAGE #35: embedded image in paragraph]

Dead-Wheel Odometry + Temporal Makers API :

The principal element of success in our autonomous mission is reliable driving trajectories. We successfully used dead wheel odometry instead of encoder based odometry, with the aid of the Roadrunner library Dead Wheel odometry allows us to reduce wheel slippage errors and also gives us sophisticated error correction during driving; giving us best in class autonomous driving capability. Especially in this season with dense junction obstacles to avoid and small margins of error. 


According to Acme robotics' documentation, here is how the readings transform to coordinates. Specifically, the position coordinates are incrementally calculated using the following matrix conversion,
[IMAGE #36: embedded image in paragraph]

We also use the Temporal Marker API from roadrunner in order to be able to simultaneously complete non-robot driving actions while driving. These include lifting the elevator and flipping the cones, in order to have a faster Teleop and be autonomous.

Finally we used the Proton multi-threaded library for precise driving and non driving controls​.

Sensors Implementations

Cone Grab Current Detector:
In autonomous, if we miss picking up a cone from the stack, lift height calibration is off for future cones and we miss other cone grabs. We needed to implement a software-based sensor to detect if the cone has been grabbed or if it has failed. So we implemented our own custom software-based sensor to detect this instead of using additional hardware like an optical sensor which might be error prone due to position.Using the Lynx module API, we are able to read raw current data from the servo. Then using a custom signal processing filter, we are able to convert this raw current data into a readable signal.  Here is the signal processing process,

[IMAGE #37: embedded image in paragraph]

If the signal is above the threshold of ~2 mA, then the signal is classified as a grabbed cone because the current draw increases when the servo stalls during the grab.


OpenCV Vision:

We use an external USB Logitech camera to detect the sleeve signal cone. We use OpenCV library for the detection of sleeve detection based on highly accurate AprilTags. We have 100% accuracy in our sleeve detection used for parking selection. ​

[IMAGE #38: embedded image in paragraph]

​AprilTags are more accurate than color- based sleeve detection as it completely avoids any inaccuracy due to background colors on the field. We have observed in previous qualifiers that this does indeed affect autonomous parking, which is a valuable mission.

State Machine for TeleOp

We had the issue that the non-driver robot actions can become complicated due to the sheer number of actions to be performed. We need to simplify and reduce the burden on drivers. So, we implemented a sophisticated state machine which eliminates 3 distinct button actions from cone grab to delivery, significantly reducing the burden on the drivers, thus reducing error. 
[IMAGE #39: embedded image in paragraph]
The state machine functions as; the lift-arm state machine uses two buttons to transition between 4 states which achieves the following: lock the cone and deliver it to the right lift junction level and drop it on the junction, and then return to initial state.



Mechanical Mathematics

[IMAGE #40: embedded image in paragraph]
Odometry Pods Spring Tension Calculation

Dead Wheel odometry is a key design feature for our autonomous missions. Reliable dead wheels need reliable contact with game field mats. To achieve this we need 2 lbs of downward force on the dead wheels which we achieved using a spring. We calculated the spring we needed by computing the spring tension specification using the formula

Force on a spring = k * d
d = distance of spring stretch
k = spring constant, lbs/in.  



Center of Gravity Calculation

One of the main problems we encountered with one of our design Iterations was the lack of balance in the robot. This design iteration had the claw attached to the arm on the same side of the robot. When the elevator extended, and the distance between the heavy claw and the lighter middle of the robot increased, the robot’s balance was lost, and it tipped over. This is where our current design is helpful, since it has an active intake and our lift on the opposite ends of the robot, we can both balance the weight, while consistently scoring with fast cycle times. 
[IMAGE #41: embedded image in paragraph]

[Heading 1] Outreach
[Heading 2] Hospital Project
Purpose:
To expose patients with fun, yet immersive STEM experience.  Many patients, especially those who have been at the hospital for a long period of time, experience negative effects.  We aim to introduce STEM and FIRST to these patients at Children’s Hospital, and perhaps eventually help contribute to research in this area too.  We want patients to be kids again, and learn and have fun through our therapeutic sessions.  We also would like to contribute to research in this area.

Research Reference:
Playing With a Purpose: The Impact of Therapeutic Recreation During Hospitalization
Jeffrey Karst, PhD (One of the primary authors).

Summary of Progress Thus Far:
We initially contacted and conversed with Jeffrey Karst (PhD) about his research into this area.  We then contacted Leslie Scott, who is a Child Life Specialist, to help us set up our curriculum and an in-person session.  For our first session, we had to make sure the content was appropriate for a large age range (5 to 18), and interactive enough to keep their attention.
Our initial task was to find an appropriate activity or kit for our first session.  This involved online research as well as in person research at Milwaukee Maker Faire (https://makerfaire.com/).  We ultimately decided on a kit from the Maker Faire called the Sound and Lights kit.  We believe this kit would be fun and engaging to all age ranges.
[IMAGE #42: embedded image in paragraph]
We then created a curriculum and presentation for the first session.  This would involve an icebreaker, slides, Introduction to FIRST, and a basic science lesson on electricity and magnetism.  We also pre-recorded the same presentation (to be posted on Youtube), so patients who couldn’t make it can still participate on their own time.  Our first in person session was on 11/22/22 at Children’s Hospital of Wisconsin.  It was a great success, and Leslie Scott said we “have sparked some future First Tech Challenge team members”.

Steps for Session Preparation:
First, we created a list of questions based on constraints, requirements, expectations, what has worked/not worked in the past. We reached out to child specialist Leslie Scott at Children’s Hospital and she answered all our questions.
We created a proposal plan for our first session. We presented this to the child specialist team at Children’s Hospital.
Bought the kit in our proposal plan, and sanitized it. 
Created presentations we would share at the event. We created a presentation about how lights and motors work so the kids will be able to understand the kits. We also created a presentation about FIRST to teach kids/parents all about it.
Using the presentations and the kit we created a video. This is mainly for virtual kids who are unable to attend the session we would be doing. It has the exact same schedule and process as what we would do in-person; it was just pre-recorded.
We did many practice runs with kids on this FTC team and gathered feedback.
On November 22nd we had our first session. It was a great experience and the kids seemed to enjoy it, saying they would love to do it again.
We then met with Dr. Karst again as well as more experts and were able to talk about more future options. This included out-patient sessions, summer camps, and even more in-person sessions.
We had our 2nd session on February 22nd, and plan on 5 more this year.

Future:
We have been re-invited for the hospital to conduct bi-monthly sessions. We will also continue to share our videos of each in-person project so hopefully other patients everywhere can participate.  We recently re-engaged with Dr. Karst, and hopefully he can help us develop a more comprehensive curriculum and other opportunities for us to explore. This includes outpatient events, such as setting up a robot field in a check-up clinic. We might introduce FLL robots at one of our upcoming sessions.  We will explore if this project can be done in an outpatient setting vs inpatient setting.  Ideally, we could help out with a study to see if STEM involvement has any beneficial effects in the long term in the lives of these kids.

STEM Fair
Our team not only wanted to impact in-patients, but also survivors of childhood illnesses, by planning a future STEM fair. After our team learned about the benefits of “play therapy” from our STEM workshop for patients at Children’s Hospital, advisors there suggested that we reach out to families with childhood illness survivors (those who are no longer hospitalized after cancer, sickle cell, and other treatments), so we planned an exciting STEM Workshop/Fair for them. We did this because many of these kids have missed meaningful opportunities that could open them up to STEM and the FIRST program, and it was important to us to make these experiences more accessible to all. We continued to plan this event with Dr. Karst and Mrs Leslie Scott, who then connected us with Chad Lawson, a childhood leukemia survivor and the founder of the non-profit organization Maker Space Ship. With his guidance, we planned 3 interactive and fun stations! A “mechanical” one, an “electrical" one (being led by Chad Lawson from Maker Space Ship and a few of our team members!), and a FIRST station where they can interact with FIRST robotics teams. We invited the First Lego League team B.U.I.L.D to co-manage the FIRST station and also provide an insight into another branch of FIRST for our participants. After that, we explored and tested different activities we could implement, and eventually decided on building a mechanical hand for one station and creating a “Space Ship”! (Maker Space Ship’s flagship craft activity!) for the other. We then reached out to all of our previous contacts from our STEM sessions as well as other experts on this subject such as Damian Buchmann, the founder of the Ability Center. Later on, we faced the challenge of not having enough sign-ups. To solve that issue, we opened up our attendee requirements to survivors of chronic conditions as well, who we realized may also be afflicted with the same lack of STEM and FIRST exposure. To relay this adjustment, we then reached out to nurses, principals, and other professionals from school districts in the area. 

[Heading 2] Computer Project: Bridging the Technology Gap

Purpose:
Over the past two years, our team has dedicated itself to an outreach project aimed at helping other FIRST teams who have limited access to technology. Our goal is to provide teams with the resources they need to effectively program their robots and use CAD software. By doing so, we aim to reduce the financial burden on teams and allow them to spend more time focusing on building and programming their robots, as well as competing in competitions to the best of their ability. Ultimately, we hope to help level the playing field and give all students an equal opportunity to explore their interests in robotics and STEM fields.

Summary of Progress Thus Far:

2021 - 2022 season:
[IMAGE #44: embedded image in paragraph]
We contacted Dan Scott, Elmbrook School Districts Director of Technology, for help on where to start with this project. He gave us great advice on how to safely and securely clean computer hard drives, reload new Windows 10, and distribute these devices to teams in need. We then contacted 9 principals in the Elmbrook School District, both the Brookfield and Elm Grove public libraries, and Beth Dobrisinski, the Executive Director of the Elmbrook Education Foundation, for help in spreading the word of our idea. All these contacts were very gracious and agreed to help us in our efforts. To start collecting the devices we set up tables at both the Brookfield and Elm Grove libraries as well as Brookfield Central and Brookfield East high schools. Once we collected a total of 78 laptops and desktops, we started to organize and sort the devices. Once we had organized all the devices we had a total of 18 usable for FLL software and 9 usable for FTC software. The other 51 devices were either sold, for the purpose of buying cases and parts for the usable computers, or responsibly recycled using the same system and the Elmbrook School District. With the 27 usable devices, we started the “DBAN” process (a secure way of wiping computers hard drives and removing the current windows on the computer). After lots of challenges, we installed a new Windows 10 Pro and the appropriate software (FLL or FTC) onto the device. Finally, we delivered devices to teams all over the Greater Milwaukee Area.  We were also featured on CBS 58 News for our work with local teams.

2022-2023 season:
In the current 2022-2023 season, our team remains dedicated to expanding our outreach efforts and reaching as many young individuals as possible. We began by completing the refurbishment of remaining computers from the previous year, and have generously donated four laptops to FIRST Lego League teams and two laptops to a FIRST Tech Challenge team.
[IMAGE #45: embedded image in paragraph]
Furthermore, we have extended our outreach to international communities, including a recent donation of five refurbished laptops to a community in Honduras. These devices were thoughtfully equipped with Spanish educational apps that encourage the development of STEM skills. We firmly believe that every individual, regardless of their location, deserves access to technology and education in STEM fields. Our contribution to the global effort to bridge the digital divide has made us incredibly proud. You can the Honduras thank you video here - 

[IMAGE #46: embedded image in paragraph]
These pictures are of the five computers that went to Honduras.

[IMAGE #47: embedded image in paragraph]
At present, we have nine additional refurbished laptops ready to be donated. Despite the challenge of finding more teams to donate to, we remain determined to identify and reach out to teams or STEM organizations who would greatly benefit from these devices.

To collect more computers to refurbish, we organized a collection drive after Christmas, targeting those in our community who may have received new devices during the holiday season. We reached out to both high schools in our school district, as well as two public libraries, and set up collection boxes at each location. Additionally, we hosted two in-person collection events at each library and distributed flyers in advance to promote the drive. This year, we specifically requested high-quality devices that would be suitable for use by teams or in STEM-related activities. The drive was successful, yielding 45 such computers. Drawing from our experiences from the past year, we have implemented various improvements to our refurbishment process and may also utilize the laptops during our CAD mentoring sessions.

[Heading 2] CAD Outreach

Purpose: 
1. Help teams to develop CAD models they need for competitions.
2. Help newcomers to develop their CAD skills
3. Make connections with our community 
[IMAGE #52: embedded image in paragraph]
Pictured:  Helping FTC Rising Rhinobots with CAD and robot design
[IMAGE #53: embedded image in paragraph]
Our idea for CAD outreach started with two previous outreach events. We were fortunate enough to have team members with experience in CAD so B.U.I.L.D. Team #44199 had asked us to help them model a battery holder. So we held a zoom meeting and modeled a specialized battery holder with them. They were able to ask about CAD and get an interactive experience which allowed them to learn the fundamentals of CAD. We helped another Brookfield FTC team the Rising Rhinobots(Team #16310) learn the basics of designing robot parts in Fusion. Overall both the outreach events were a major success, and we realized that we could have a pretty big impact on our community by doing this. Since we wanted to continue this impact we created a CAD outreach subteam that was dedicated to teaching kids. This was the start of our CAD outreach project.






CAD Session Design Process:
1. To start off we discussed our target audience. This is important, because different age groups learn in different ways. There are other factors besides age that we had to consider such as the interest level of the age group. After discussion we decided that we wanted to aim our CAD outreach project to Fll teams. The kids in the FLL program are probably more interested in CAD, along with that students in FLL will need to know the basics of CAD if they plan on doing FTC.  This is part of our sustainability plan, because teaching FLL kids important skills such as CAD will help them succeed in FTC, and later in their STEM careers.
2. After we get an idea of our audience we start by researching different CAD project ideas, so we have a general idea for the curriculum. We look at a variety of factors when deciding which CAD model to pick. We needed to consider the difficulty level of the project, and the skills students could learn from these projects. We also have to keep our audience in mind when doing this since a younger audience may have trouble understanding more complex CAD ideas. All of these ideas are discussed and then put into a sheet which we can use as a template for our presentation.
3. Before we start making the presentation we make sure that every team member presenting knows all the content, and has the skills to create the project by themselves. To achieve this we make sure that the CAD outreach team members create the projects separately. Along with that we made sure that every CAD outreach team member had taken an online CAD course so they know the basic, and complex ideas of CAD before they start teaching. Overall we make sure that all team members practice the projects we will be teaching and that they know the content inside and out to ensure the highest quality CAD session possible.
4. Once everyone has a knowledge of all the content and projects we start developing our presentation. We want to make the presentation have a lot of information, but also for it to be easy to understand. To achieve this we make sure that most slides have an annotated picture that shows the step, along with a short easy to follow description that explains what to do. In the beginning of our slideshow we also include a “basic skills” section which gives an overview of the skills that the students will be learning. During this step we hold multiple meetings together to check in on our progress and discuss additional ideas.
 5. Finally once our slideshow is finished we start to decide our organization or “roles” for the presentation. Since there is a lot going on during a session it is important to make sure that everyone has a “job” to keep the presentation running smoothly. Generally we have 1 person using the computer to model what is happening in the slideshow. Then we have 1 person presenting the slideshow, and finally we have two “helpers” who assist the students throughout the session. During a presentation the presenter talks about the slide and what to do, then the person on the computer models what the presenter says and finally the 2 helpers walk around and answer any questions that the students may have.
[IMAGE #54: embedded image in paragraph]
 (CAD outreach presentation design process)


How we educated our Students:

1. We give an overview of the presentations and what roles we will have. During this introduction part we show the students the skills they will learn during the presentation. Additionally we point out what roles the team members will have. This lets the students know who they can ask for help, who is presenting, and who is modeling the presentation. Finally  we make sure to mention how the presentation will flow(as described above) and how every step includes an annotated picture and text description.

2. We go through an "essential skills" section. The purpose of which is to expose the students to any necessary skills before we start working on the project. During this section, we will model skills such as sketching, extruding, or more complex skills such as patterning, depending on the group and their experience with CAD. We give the students an opportunity to practice these skills so that their transition into making projects can be easier. We also teach any necessary shortcuts during this section, which can make navigating Fusion much easier.

3. We go through the projects. This is the longest section in our presentations and covers a wide variety of important Fusion 360 skills. We start off with a simple project that covers some of the easiest techniques covered in the “essential skills” section. We try to keep the presentation at a pace that the students can follow and we include checkpoints so that we can make sure that every student keeps up. After the simple project we present a more complex project that uses some of the more advanced skills mentioned in our “essential skills” section. The purpose of this project is to challenge the students and give them an opportunity to apply the knowledge they learned throughout the session. Additionally if they're ever stuck they can always ask for help since we always have 2 people available.

4. To finish off we have offer student choice time. This section happens at the end of the presentation and the students can make whatever they want. The purpose of this section is for the student to apply what they learned to whatever they are interested in. We also encourage the students to ask questions during this section so that they can increase their knowledge of CAD even further, and we offer a form a student can fill out if they are interested in 3D printing their creations.
[IMAGE #55: embedded image in paragraph]
Benefits of our Outreach project:

1. We were able to connect to our community by educating kids interested in CAD. Teaching kids these skills that are required for FTC would encourage them to join an FTC team in the future. So doing CAD outreach events not only allows us to connect to our community but it also allows us to spread the impact of FIRST in our community.

2. We were able to build on our team's knowledge of CAD. The team members of the CAD outreach team got the opportunity to practice and apply their CAD skills in real world situations. This allowed us to learn fundamental CAD skills which would come in use next season.

3. We were able to give kids an opportunity to learn CAD.. Many students are interested in learning CAD especially when learning about its potential applications, but are unable to because many CAD programs such as Fusion 360 have many features which may overwhelm new users. Our CAD course was able to break down these complicated features into simpler ones allowing students to truly understand how to use the software without being overwhelmed.             
[IMAGE #56: embedded image in paragraph]
The Future of our CAD outreach program:

Our CAD project is still in its early stages and we plan on increasing its impact to more FLL teams globally and even to middle school students. We as a team have been very fortunate to have the skills necessary for CAD and our goal is to teach these skills to interested students so that they can apply these valuable skills later on in life. We have already been communicating with FTC teams who might be interested and we plan on starting a CAD summer program to increase our impact on the community.

[Heading 1] Award Descriptions
[Heading 2] Control

Autonomous objectives:
Our objective for the autonomous mode was to develop op modes which were consistent, reliable, high scoring, and configurable for compatibility with multiple alliance teams. We followed a very disciplined game matrix process to select the appropriate autonomous challenges, which gave us the most points for a manageable level of difficulty. We aim for a 1 + 5 autonomous on medium and high junctions, with parking in 30 seconds giving us 45-50 points with 95% reliability.
Sensors used:

[IMAGE #57: embedded image in paragraph]
Cone Grab Current Sensor 
Problem: In autonomous, if we miss picking up a cone from the stack, lift height calibration is off for future cones and we miss other cone grabs.  Solution: We needed to implement a software-based sensor to detect if the cone has been grabbed or if it has failed. We implemented our own custom software-based sensor to detect this instead of using additional hardware like an optical sensor which might be error prone due to position. Implementation: Using the Lynx module API, we are able to read raw current data from the servo. Then using a custom signal processing filter, we are able to convert this raw current data into a readable signal. If the signal is above the threshold of ~2 mA, then the signal is classified as a grabbed cone because the current draw increases when the servo stalls during the grab.

Dead Wheel Odometry + Roadrunner Library
Problem: In the 30 second autonomous period, we need to get precise pathing using splines  & have some level of drive error correction to accomplish reliable missions. Solution: Our three dead wheel odometry pods allow us to get precise location of our robot on the field for reliable driving trajectories with the aid of the Roadrunner Library.  Implementation: We used dead wheel odometry to reduce wheel slippage errors and give us sophisticated error correction during autonomous, so we can use precise pathing. 

Camera/OpenCV 
Problem: We need the ability to detect parking position based on the cone sleeve. Solution: We use the OpenCV library detecting custom April Tag based images for our custom sleeve. Implementation: We use an external USB Logitech camera for a sleeve detection system, and we use OpenCV to detect the April Tag in our custom sleeve. This method results in a reliable detection system, and has 100% accuracy in our testing practices.
Key algorithms:
[IMAGE #58: embedded image in paragraph]
PIDF + Motion Profiling + Differential Elevator
[IMAGE #59: embedded image in paragraph]
Problem: We need fast, reliable, and controlled lift and arm mechanism for our TeleOP and Autonomous state machines, where simple encoder-based designs are too imprecise. Solution: We implemented our own Proportional Integral Derivative Feed-forward control mechanism along with a custom motion profiling algorithm, to precisely control the lift and arm system. This gives us control over position, velocity, and acceleration. Implementation: Implemented PIDF and motion profiling. We used a trapezoidal profile in v (velocity) vs. t (time), where the first phase has maximum acceleration, the second has maximum velocity, and the final phase has maximum deceleration. Taking the integral achieves the x (position) vs. t (time), of the elevator and rotational parameter of the arm, which is used by the robot PID controller to iteratively correct itself. The feed-forward aspect of the PIDF control is used to correct the gravitational force errors.

Driver controlled enhancements:
State Machine
[IMAGE #60: embedded image in paragraph]
Problem: The non-driver robot actions can become complicated due to the sheer number of actions to be performed. We need to simplify and reduce the burden on drivers. Solution: We implemented a sophisticated state machine which eliminates 3 distinct button actions from cone grab to delivery, significantly reducing the burden on the drivers, thus reducing error. Implementation: The lift-arm state machine uses two buttons to transition between 4 states which achieves the following: lock the cone and deliver it to the right lift junction level and drop it on the junction, and then return to initial state.
[IMAGE #61: embedded image in paragraph]
Precision Field of View (FOV) Driver Control
Problem: In TeleOP driving, robot centric driving adds complexity and burden on the drivers Solution: We need to convert the robots movements from robot centric to driver centric in order for the robot to move relative to the drivers reference frame. Implementation: We used trigonometry for the robot to move relative to the driver’s view independent of the robot's orientation on the field which unburdens the driver and simplifies the driving in TeleOp. 
[IMAGE #62: embedded image in paragraph]
Cone Guidance Mechanism
Problem: In autonomous mode where we are trying to grab and deliver 5 cones, The cone grab position is error prone and yields reliability of only 60% in the mission. Solution: We implemented a cone guidance system in the base of the robot to guide the cone in the right grab position and increase our reliability to 95%+. This complements the software part of the control system with simplicity and makes it less prone to failures. Implementation: We 3D printed cone guides to be attached to the base of the robot which guide the cone in the right grabbing position. This provides lateral tolerance of +-3 inches thus improving our autonomous mode reliability significantly by +35%. This is a great example of software and mechanical control systems working together with simplicity to improve precision and reliability.

Junction Guards 
Problem: In TeleOP and autonomous, when delivering the cones to the junction, lining up with the junction is often difficult. Solution: We need to have a guide that lines up our robot with the junction for reliable delivery. Implementation: We have 2 plates that automatically flip down and guide our robot to the optimal delivery position without being penalized every time we deliver a cone. The plates are operated by the same set of motors controlling the Differential Elevator.

Engineering portfolio references:

[TABLE START]
Subject | Page Number(s)

Software Design Process - ​​Cone Grab Current Sensor | Page 9
Software Design Highlights​ - Diver FOV, OpenCV, Dead Wheel Odometry, State Machine | Page 10
Mathematics of our Robot -  Driver FOV, Odometry pod, PIDF & Motion Profiling Mathematics | Page 11
[TABLE END]
[IMAGE #63: embedded image in paragraph]
Autonomous program diagrams:
We developed two autonomous modes this year which do the following, (1) scan sleeve (2) score the preloaded cone in high junction (3) for the rest of autonomous we intake from the stack and score in high junction, or middle junction for the second opmode. In a perfect autonomous we can cycle 5 times (4) and finally park based on the scanned sleeve. The possible number of points we can get are 45 - 50 in these op-modes.


[Heading 2] Connect
One of our primary goals this season was to connect with as many experts in Science, Technology, Engineering, and Mathematics in our state, nationally, and internationally. We have come to understand how important it is to have connections that can teach our team about how STEM skills are used in the 21st-century workforce. In addition, to these community connections, we strived to connect people, specifically students, who might need access to FIRST and STEM educations and opportunities our team has been blessed to have. 
This year we have worked with more than 40 community connections; these individuals span multiple industries and 20 different companies and organizations. Our community connections have allowed us to elevate all aspects of our season, from the robot to editing and building a website. Out of our authentic connections this season, we connected with an additional 12 community members for the first time. Each one of these community members was new to FIRST and what FIRST was about, and by becoming a connection with the GEarheads, we were able to enhance these community connections’ knowledge about the resources and work that FIRST, and more specifically, FTC teams do.

[Heading 2] Motivate

Organization
In this third season for FTC, we really wanted to refine our team composition and sustainability. The biggest change we brought this year was that we defined a more formal team charter & roles; also we had everyone apply for those roles based on their interest, expertise and willingness to learn. This was followed by interviews with coaches.  We got lots of interest from our expo events, and we refined our team composition. 
We are very proud of our 4 new student mentors who truly have made their impact felt. The way in which the team members applied for these roles is, each of them labeled the top 3 sections they were interested in participating in. We ended up talking about it as a team, and getting the coaches input to create a final spreadsheet. We used this process in many other cases, such as ranking the different outreach projects from least to most important, and making these decisions about what we should participate in as a team. 
fvcvfSame as last year, we had a main goal to restructure our team so that every single person had their role on the team. Each position had a particular set of tasks that needed to be achieved. Another way we stretched our organization into the community was having each unique role make a Gantt chart with the tasks that needed to be done and made it much easier to complete and further the reach of our team as we were able to get more things done without losing process and goals throughout the season. 

[IMAGE #66: embedded image in paragraph]
The screenshot above shows a snippet of what we did to rank our priorities as a team, and how we took decisions. . .to prevent arguments, and to map out what everyone had in mind.

[IMAGE #67: embedded image in paragraph]
The screenshot above, shows the team rankings after everyone voted for what they wanted to do, and the green highlighted boxes, represent the final decision, after the coaches provided their input.  It also  displays the different roles of team members, and their participation.

Sprint Plan:

[IMAGE #68: embedded image in paragraph]
This year, our team used the sprint plan process to complete tasks efficiently and to keep each other accountable for the work that has to be completed. We started off by setting 2 week time frames, and each of the student leaders would note down what tasks have to be completed by the end of the 2 week time frame. Then, at the end of the 2 weeks, the spreadsheet with everyone’s progress would be locked, and then we would go over what each of us did as a team. For anyone who did not complete the tasks they said they would complete, they would have a conversation with the coach to make sure they knew what the expectations are, and if they could not meet those expectations a second or third time, then they would be removed from the team, and a student mentor would join the team. In previous years, there were many people who were excited about robotics and STEM, but could not join FTC Teams because of the 15 person limit. That’s why, this year we introduced the student mentor route. Anyone who was interested in FTC or Robotics could be a student mentor, and help the team with different tasks, and be part of the team. But, they wouldn’t be officially registered as team members. This helped expand our team and First, giving more kids more opportunities. It also helped our sustainability, in the sense that if any team members left or did not follow the sprint plan rules, a student mentor could replace them. 
The screenshot above shows the first sprint plan. Majority of tasks have been completed, and with time we have gotten better, and better at completing tasks in the time frame given.

[IMAGE #69: embedded image in paragraph]
Team Identity
GEarheads as a team has been maturing.  In all four years as an FTC team, we qualified for Wisconsin State championship.  We started our journey with just making our first robot; today we know we have made progress in our design and software; our engineering process with documentation is more advanced. We are especially proud of the outreach impact we have made with under-resourced teams in Milwaukee (MKE), and at the Childrens Hospital of Wisconsin. For the next two years, we want to expand FTC in our schools to bring more kids to FIRST. With that collective strength, we also hope to bring more FIRST/STEM opportunities to the underprivileged parts of the Milwaukee school district, and continue to expand our Hospital Outreach Project. We will continue to participate in meaningful outreach projects.
Our team follows a simple mantra, and this is the main core values of our team:

We show this by learning from our past experiences, participating in meaningful outreach, and constantly improving our team, teamwork, and decision making skills. Also learning from professionals.

Goals:
Communication: : We will grow our team brand, and our ability to give back to the FIRST community by improving our communication focus through the web and social media. This includes posting regularly on social media platforms such as Instagram, Twitter, and also updating our website regularly to keep information accurate.

Organization: To achieve the goals we have set as a team, organization and keeping each other accountable for the work that has to be completed is key.

Documentation: Through past experiences, we learned that documentation is not something to put off to the last minute, and that being able to convey the work we did properly was a skill we would need to improve. So, we would like to improve our Engineering Notebook, Portfolio, and overall documentation.

Competitive Robot: We will continue to build super competitive robots as a way to showcase our team technical capability

Outreach and Community Impact: We will focus our outreach on enabling the growth of new teams by providing resources to start up teams, and by developing and growing technical skills - sharing that learning with the FTC community. 

Sustainability/Team Selection Process:
As a 6th year FIRST team (2 FLL + 4 FTC), we understand that we need to be able to retain existing members and attract new team members, especially as existing members graduate or move to FRC teams.
We applied our sustainability plan in two ways. (1) actively recruiting new team members, via FIRST/STEM Expos and  (2) spending time with younger FLL teams, in order to inspire them to go into FTC once they enter their late middle school and early high school years.

[IMAGE #70: embedded image in paragraph]
This year we have recruited 4 new student members, Rhea, John, Victor, and Shashank. These new student members have been improving the team with mechanical work, software, and major outreach projects such as the Childrens hospital.



People:
The main 2 methods we used to sustain our team, was through FLL Mentoring and First Outreach Events. For the past 2 years, we have been mentoring FLL Teams, and after they complete FLL, they will feed into our FTC Team as a next step. In addition, during our First Outreach events, we talk about our team, and if they are interested they can apply. The next step is filling out the application form. On that we questioned their interest, commitment, and skill. From there, we selected 15 people to be on the team, and the rest could be student mentors, so they still had an opportunity to explore STEM.
```

### Wisconsin State Qualifier Judging Presentation.docx

- **Original filename:** `Wisconsin State Qualifier Judging Presentation.docx`
- **File size:** 199 KB
- **Document type:** Word document — judging presentation script
- **Season/Year:** 2022-23 (Power Play)
- **Category:** Judging Materials
- **Rev 4, Updated March 6, 2023**
- **Team stated as '6th year being a FIRST team, 4th year in FTC'**
- **Structured as a timed speaking script (per-section time allocations, e.g. 'Time 12 seconds') with named speakers (Sid, Vaidehi, Josef, etc.)**
- **Covers Computer Outreach / STEM Access project (11 events, 149 devices collected)**

**Complete extracted content (verbatim):**

```text
[core_properties] title='Word Document' author='' created=None modified=datetime.datetime(2026, 7, 2, 19, 51, 37, tzinfo=datetime.timezone.utc) last_modified_by='python-docx'



[Heading 3] GEarheads Judging Presentation
[normal] Rev 4
[normal] Updated March 6, 2023

[Heading 3] Introduction Hello - Sid

[normal] Sid Hello judges, we would like to start with a 5-minute presentation, would that be all right?

[Heading 3] 1. Introduction - Sid (Time 12 seconds)

[normal] We are team 16460 the GEarheads, this is our 6th year being a FIRST team, and 4th year in FTC.  We would like to share our goals and accomplishments this year with you.


[normal] 2 - Goals – Vaidehi (TIME: 14 seconds)
[Heading 3] As a team, our three main goals this season were to:
[normal] Improve our planning and build a more structured team organization.
[normal] Grow our community connections bringing our learnings back to the FIRST community.
[normal] and, Continue to drive more impactful outreach into the STEM community.

[normal] We will begin with Outreach.


[Heading 3] 3 –Computer Outreach / STEM Access - Josef (Time: 50 seconds)

[normal] We continued our very successful Computer Outreach Project, which involved working with more than a dozen community leaders and hosting 11 events to collect 149 devices. 

[normal] We have refurbished, repaired, and delivered 19 laptops this season, bringing us to a total of 44 laptops overall, to help close technology access gaps for hundreds of students per year - impacting 34 FIRST teams in need, STEAM Milwaukee, and even a school in Honduras. 



[normal] Providing tech access is important us, we dedicated 440 hours over two seasons to this project.  

[Heading 3] 4 –Hospital Outreach Highlights  - Rhea (Time: 50 seconds)

[normal] One of the most meaningful projects we have done is to bring “play therapy” for kids undergoing long-term hospitalization and treatment, using STEM and FIRST.

[normal] We worked with numerous experts including Dr. Jeffrey Karst and Leslie Scott, Child Life Specialist at Childrens Hospital, to guide us. We connected with eBlox at Milwaukee Maker Fair and bought Lights and Motion kits, to teach with.

[normal] So far, we have held 2 in-person and live-streamed STEM workshops at Childrens to 21 patients.  The kids’ energy levels improved and many of them smiled and laughed!  Due to the impact and popularity of these sessions, we have been invited to host 5 more sessions through December.  Many patients and parents asked how they could join FIRST.

[normal] At the suggestion of experts at Children’s, we also developed an outpatient STEM Fair which we will deploy later this year.

[normal] We have invested over 260 hours towards this project so far.

[Heading 3] 5.  Connecting with STEM Experts – Misha (Time: 55 seconds)

[normal] We have connected with over 40 STEM leaders this year.   Our focus was to connect and share their expertise with others in the FIRST community.

[normal] First we toured Milwaukee Tool with Chief Engineer Daniel Medeiros to learn about user centered design.
[normal] We also toured the GE Healthcare Additive Manufacturing Lab with Chief Engineer Jimmie Beecham to learn how GE is accelerating it’s business with additive manufacturing.
[normal] To reach a broad audience, we hosted 2 webinars.  First with Vijay Sankaran, CTO of Johnson Controls, who spoke about disruptive innovation, and the second with Ashish Udani, a Senior Engineer at Midwest Engineered Systems who spoke about careers in robotics and automation. 


[normal] 6. Design Process - Avaneesh (Time: 20 seconds )

[normal] We have learned from these mentors.   One of the most important is a Design Process that came from GE called Design History Framework.  

[normal] We start considering robot requirements with a Game Points matrix and identify scoring capabilities and how difficult they are to implement.  We then develop and test subsystems for each of these functions, using 100% CAD design to review those ideas.

[normal] Let’s talk more about our iterations and innovations.

[Heading 3] 7 Design Iterations – Matthew (Time: 40)

[normal] We have built 4 robots this season. The first 2 came from the Homestead 30 hour build challenge. We shared our learnings with a wide audience on a FIRST live stream and our YouTube channel – driving over 35,000 views.

[normal] Our third robot was our primary robot for all qualifiers this season.  It has a unique sweeping intake mechanism on one end that delivers cones to a gripper and elevator on the other side. It was so innovative that an FRC team asked us to share it with them.
[normal] Although it was innovative, we noticed many unfixible flaws with this robot. We needed to change directions.

[Heading 3] 8 Robot Design – Thomas (Time: 35 seconds)
[Heading 3] We took the challenge to build a completely new 4th robot late in the season.

[normal] We optimized our drive train with dead wheel odometry to work with a lighter, faster, and smaller robot.
[normal] We improved our cone gripper with a geometry that is able to self-center, and detect the cones using a current sensing algorithm.
[normal] We rebuilt a lighter and faster elevator using lift belts, minimized the structure, and strengthened it with truss bracing.
[normal] We solved the problem of gripper positioning speed with a differential drive mechanism that runs the elevator as well as the arm positioning with a single set of motors, all located at the base of the robot to keep the center of gravity low.


[Heading 3] 9 Mechanical Design and Fabrication – Matthew (Time: 30 seconds)
[Heading 3] We built this 4th robot in just 4 weeks. transitioning from a full robot CAD model directly into 3D printing, CNC, and manual fabrication. This includes making a full robot CAD model, 3d printing and CNCing parts, and finally assembly.

[normal] We added new CNC capability this year constructing an “MPCNC” mill from an open source design.  We envisioned this project as a way to show other FTC teams that they could learn CNC. It also allows us fabricate alluminum parts much faster than before.
[Heading 3] 11 Software Design Process – Kartik (Time: 42 seconds)

[normal] Our software process was stretched this year to work through 4 major robot iterations, and to manage the complexity of the robot we used in the qualifiers.

[normal] Our State Machine simplifies driving.   The code has a model of actions that happen one after another, and we cycle through each action with a button press.

[normal] FOV transfers the frame of reference of the robot to the driver's frame of reference.  This is done by using a continuously updated trigonometric calculation to update orientation from the on board gyro.

[normal] PID Plus Motion Profiling Controls our Differential Elevator Drive.   Using two nested PID control loops, motion that is common between two motors drives the elevator up and down.  Motion that is differential across these motors rotates our horizontal arm.  This required us to implement two complimentary PIDF control algorithms.


[Heading 3] 12 - Living FIRST Values and Investing in FIRST – Thomas (Time: 45 seconds)

[normal] We know we can learn and solve problems.  The powerful part happens when we bring it back to the FIRST community.  

[normal] FLL mentoring included volunteering at explore events, demonstrating at scrimmages, volunteering at the Franklin regional, and mentoring 3 FLL teams – taking a lead role with 57921.  We coached them through building, 3D printing, and team organization. We invested nearly 250 hours in FLL this season.
[normal] From mentoring we saw the need for an early CAD program.  We developed and deployed an in person CAD course to more than 35 students.
[normal] Our outreach to FTC continues to expand and a growing number of teams that have reached out to learn.  

[normal] Thank you, we will be glad to take any questions.





[normal] TOTAL ~6.5 Minutes…..  Test on Monday and discuss material to cut




[normal] Judging Question Prep


[normal] What have you done to drive sustainability of your team?



[normal] Tell me about the community connections you have leveraged for your computer project?



[normal] How do you identify teams that need computers and how do you know it has an impact?



[normal] How did you come up with the idea of “Play Therapy” for your hospital outreach?



[normal] What impact has the hospital outreach project had?



[normal] You brought on 4 new team members; how did you ramp them up on your team?



[normal] Why did you end up building a new robot for state, what drove that and what did you learn?



[normal] Tell me about the innovations in your robot?



[normal] How does the “differential drive” work and why did you need it?



[normal] Tell me about the control methods and sensors you have in your robot?



[normal] How did you build a new robot so quickly?











[Heading 3] 3 –FIRST and STEM Outreach – Who (Time: ?)

[normal] EXECUTIVE SUMMARY – SHORTEN THIS INTRO PIECE

[normal] We expanded our FIRST and STEM outreach efforts significantly this year through 3 major initiatives:
[normal] 1. Mentored and supported teams at ALL levels of FIRST, including weekly mentoring of XXXX, connecting with hundreds/thousands of people at FLL and FTC scrimmages, qualifiers, and ?? (CAN WE QUANTIFY ## OF EVENTS, HOURS, ETC? – Sid Avaneesh)
[normal] Created and hosted a series of in-person CAD courses for FLL teams (~50 hours), as part of our sustainability efforts to grow future FTC and FRC members
[normal] Build a MP CNC (took ~50 hours)
[normal] 2. Continued our very successful Computer Repurposing Project, which involved working with XX community leaders to collect 125 devices (45 since January).  We have refurbished delivered 25 laptops to help close technology access gaps for 9 FLL and FTC teams in need in Milwaukee, a school in Honduras, and STEAM Milwaukee.
[normal] Provided “play therapy” to cancer patients at Children’s Hospital, by developing and delivering an on-going STEM Workshop series with mentoring from doctors and Child Life Specialists.

[normal] FIRST ENGAGEMENT incl CAD WOKSHOP GOES HERE…



[normal] Experts at Children’s also suggested that we develop a STEM Fair for outpatient Childhood Illness Survivors. So, we reserved a venue for March 4th, developed hands-on activities, recruited 2 FLL teams, and secured Chad Lawson from Maker Space Ship. Following HIPAA restrictions, we worked through Children’s and Principals and Nurses and 3 school districts (Elmbrook, New Berlin and Wauwatosa) to promote it. Unfortunately, there were not enough registrations, so we will be rescheduling it.
```

```text
GE Healthcare’s DHF Engineering Process
We leveraged professional design history file (DHF) framework from GE Healthcare used in developing reliable medical products like MRI, CT machines. This design process improved design & prototyping  efficiency and reliability by using subsystem-based design and integration. This, along with our 2-week sprint plans, helps us design, iterate and improve all aspects of our season – robot, outreach and docs.
Early phases of the Design Process - Game points Matrix
Game points matrix is the first and most important step of our design flow down. By  
 analyzing the risks and rewards of each mission, we can translate it down in user and 
subsystem requirements. We translate these subsystem requirements into a design goals. 
Later phases of the Design process- Robot Design, Fabrication, Integration

Industry-based Engineering Design Process - DHF
After the goals and requirements are documented, we design our robot on CAD using Fusion 360. We use the CAD designs for the individual subsystems, apply industrial fabrication tools like 3D printing, CNC milling (we use our custom-made CNC), and manual sheet metal manufacturing to build the robot. We then integrate each robot subsystem with subsystem software, then extensively test and optimize through testing, scrimmages and qualifiers.

How we Iterate so quickly? : To iterate upon our designs, our team rigidly adheres to a system of learning from previous designs, game strategies, and driver experience on the field. Once a robot has been completed, our team goals and strategies constantly iterated, which helps us iterate our robot design. For example, the main design requirement that prompted the build of our Mark II robot was the realization that fallen cones were not nearly as important as we thought they would be, and that we needed a faster cycle time. To meet these design requirements, a new robot was 3D modeled using CAD and fusion 360 and was built in a week using industrial design and fabrication processes. We completed software using subsystem based design in parallel which took us just under one week.
Iterative Design | Disciplined Learning Process | Continuous Improvement

Innovation in Robotics: Differential Lift-Arm
Innovative Lift + Arm + Claw mechanism | Mechanical Innovation | State-of-the-art Software Control
INNOVATEing to deliver maximum performance with minimal hardware
Differential Controls
Differential Mechanical Design
Differential Lift and Arm 
One of our main design goals for Mark-II  robot was to have a symmetric/dual sided pickup and drop capability, minimal weight ,  high speed, and simple wiring. We achieved this with our innovative & effective differential mechanism, which utilizes just two motors to power both the lift vertically and deposit arm horizontally in beautiful synchronization. Both motors are situated at the base of the robot to lower the center of gravity. 
The mechanism translates the sum (v1 + v2) of the motor velocities into lift motion, and the difference (v1- v2) into arm motion. This allows us to devote both motors to either mechanism or share power in between them to achieve the minimum time to the desired position. 
The basic principle of the differential design is that of opposing forces. When the motors move in the same direction, the torques on the arm pivot oppose, so the lift must move up. When the motors move in opposite directions, the forces on the lift cancel and cause the arm to move angularly. To smoothly transfer the motors’ torque to both the lift and arm, we utilized custom belt-driven linear slides. 
        The biggest challenge in the design was achieving high torque for the arm while having high speed for the lift. This was accomplished through a series of compound belt reductions which were carefully designed to provide maximum torque and range of motion for the arm. A 2.6:1 reduction from the motor pulley to the large arm pulley combined with a 1.7:1 reduction from the large pulley to the arm pivot provides over 60 kg.cm of torque to the arm while giving the slides a maximum speed of over 2500 mm/s.
This differential mechanism requires equally sophisticated controls & tuning to achieve its full potential. We utilize custom developed dual PIDF (Proportional, Integral, Derivative & Feed forward) controller and trapezoidal motion profile. 
        The ingenuity of the design is in delivering both degrees of freedom at once for lift and arm; implemented via a power-sharing scheme to obtain the fastest combined motion. We empirically determined the maximum speed and power used by the lift and arm individually.  We used sophisticated PIDF tunning procedures to achieve this refined movement.
When they are set to move at the same time, we determine the factors by which their motions must be slowed so that they stop at the same time and the power used does not exceed the maximum power of the motors.

DESIGNing the best
DESIGNing the best
Mechanical Design & Fabrication
For complex parts with many features, and for prototype parts, we chose 3D printing to quickly build a real-life model. Using newly adapted parameters for the 3D printers, our team can more quickly make stronger and more accurate parts.
“Cardboard Aided Manufacturing”
Cardboard Aided Manufacturing  is our primary fabrication technique for machining sheet metal parts by hand. By utilizing 1:1 scaled paper from our CAD model, an accurate model of the outline of a part along with any holes and features inside can be glued onto aluminum. 
CNC Milling & MPCNC Mill
Because our robot is made up of mostly custom machined parts, the build process is quite expensive and takes a much longer time than usual. To mitigate this issue, our team has created a prototype system that utilizes cheap and easy-to-fabricate materials, such as laser-cut wood and 3D-printed parts, to make sure that the larger parts of the robot fit together well. This is not used for small linkages and assemblies, as they are small enough that changing them is not costly in time or money.
Prototyping our Robot
We used Fusion 360 to CAD design our whole robot in the first few weeks after kickoff. Using CAD allowed us to create custom parts using methods like 3D printing or CNC machining, giving much more design flexibility than a solely kit-based approach.
We organized our CAD design into subsystems and used joints to define how the parts would move. This allowed us to confirm that our design could accomplish all of the planned objectives and make sure that the moving parts had enough space. Using joints also allowed us to experiment with more complex features like linkages, which we used effectively. 
Having a 100% CAD Design lets us employ an increased amount of custom fabrication methods that are pointed to in this page.

In autonomous, the height of our lift is dependent on the number of cones left in the stack.  Because of this, missing one grab can cause the rest of the cycles to fail. To mitigate this failure mode, we sense the current of the claw servo after the grab to determine if it is stalled (indicating a successful grab). The raw data is very noisy due to an error in the firmware, so we compute a moving average over 50 data points.
We analyzed design failure modes & added effective mitigations to improve performance and reliability of our robot.

Trigonometry in TeleOp | Vector Math in Dead wheel Odometry | Computer Vision
State Machine for TeleOp
Computer Vision
Driver Centric Driving
We implemented a state machine in our TeleOp program which allows for effortless control. The lift arm state machine transitions between 4 main states: locking the cone, delivering to the right lift junction level, dropping it, and then returning to initial state. Our state machine allows us to traverse all these states with just 2 buttons. This greatly enhances our TeleOp capability and decreases the difficulty of driving. 
We used sophisticated trigonometry to implement our TeleOP driving which is configured to the point of view of the driver. With this, the robot always move relative to the driver’s view independent of robot’s orientation on the field which unburdens the driver and greatly simplifies the driving in TeleOp. 
We use an external USB Logitech camera to detect the sleeve signal cone. We use OpenCV library for the detection for sleeve detection based in highly accurate AprilTags. We have 100% accuracy in our sleeve detection used for parking selection. 
AprilTags are more accurate than color- based sleeve detection as it completely avoids any inaccuracy due to background colors on the field. This method results in a reliable detection system and has 100% accuracy in our testing practices.
[IMAGE #78: Google Shape;3596;p10]
Controlling Innovatively and Efficiently
[IMAGE #79: Google Shape;3598;p10]
Software Design Highlights
Dead wheel Odometry
The principal element of success in our autonomous mission is reliable driving trajectories. We successfully used dead wheel odometry instead of encoders based odometry, with the aid of the Roadrunner library.
Dead Wheel odometry allows us to reduce wheel slippage errors and also gives us sophisticated error correction during driving. We also use the Temporal Marker API from roadrunner and Proton multi-threaded library for precise driving and non driving controls

Field-Centric Driving Trigonometry
Letting tempForwardPower and tempSidePower be the values given by the gamepad, and angle represent the angle turned by the orbot measured by the gyroscopic sensor, we calculate the inputs for the robot as forwardPower and sidePower given by,
The robot always moves relative to the driver’s view independent of robot’s orientation on the field which greatly aids the driver. We used trigonometry to implement our TeleOP driving which is configured to the point of view of the driver.  To do this we need to convert the coordinates of the robot into that of the field, to do so consider the following. 
We implemented a custom PIDF (Proportional, Integral, Derivative, Feedforward) controller to control our motors. We realize that there are open source solutions for this, but we wanted the flexibility of a custom controller. Some new features include a fully customizable feedforward function based on position, velocity, and acceleration, as well as resetting the integral term to avoid integral windup.
In addition, we used motion profiling to control our lift (see pg.XX). Motion profiling generates a state of position, velocity, and acceleration at a given time that limits maximum velocity and acceleration while moving to the target as fast as possible. The main type we used, trapezoidal motion profiling, takes its name from the trapezoidal velocity vs. time graph. Like the PIDF controller, we chose to take the custom route on our motion profiling. This allowed us to make motion profiles chainable for easy control, and to implement different types of motion profiles such as delays or timed profiles (where the motion takes place over a predetermined time frame instead of as fast as possible).
[IMAGE #89: Google Shape;3633;p11]
[IMAGE #90: Google Shape;3634;p11]
Code snippet of retrieving (x) position from motion profile.
[IMAGE #91: Google Shape;3636;p11]
Mathematics of our Robot
INNOVATE -ing to push the envelope
Odometry Pods Spring Tension Calculation
Deadwheel odometry is a key design feature for our autonomous missions. Reliable deadwheels need reliable contact with game field mats. To achieve this we need 2 lbs of downward force on the deadwheels which we achieved using a spring.
We calculated the spring we needed by computing the spring tension specification using the formula
[IMAGE #92: Google Shape;3642;p11]
Force on a spring = k * d
d = distance of spring stretch
k = spring constant, lbs/in.  
Requires a spring with a spring constant value  of 8.3 pounds per inch or greater
Graph of a trapezoidal profile
[IMAGE #93: Google Shape;3647;p11]

[layout: 2_Title and Content - No Rule]
FIRST Mentoring & CAD Outreach
Team 1640 GEarheads | Innovation | Impact | Inclusion | Teamwork | Fun 
Sharing Expertise to Grow FIRST Team Skills and Continuing Impactful Outreach
CONNECTing with our community
12
We are a team that has grown greatly with the help of our mentors in terms of our technical skills and our outreach. We have made it our goal to pay this forward by mentoring and growing the FIRST community around us. 
FRC: We are very proud of the fact that an award winning FRC team (2202 BEAST robotics), reached out to us to collaborate on the design of their robot’s intake. We were invited to one of their meetings, and we displayed and explained the innovative mechanisms of our Mark I robot, which they were going to base their design on. 
FLL: FLL mentoring is a big part of both our team’s sustainability and helping grow the FIRST community around us. We mentored 3 FLL teams this year, Team B.U.I.L.D. (44199), team Stark Energy (57921), and FLL Lightning Bolts (53650). All teams qualified for the State Championship and team BUILD is moving onto Worlds. We mentored these teams in CAD design, their project, and their robot design. We helped team B.U.I.L.D with their innovation project., from how to approaching design, to CAD, and all the way to 3D printing. Along with mentoring, we also created our own CAD course. (the impact of this is described in the section to the right)
FTC: As a team with 4 years of experience in FTC, we are constantly trying to help grow the FTC community around us. We helped a team from our own school district, 16310 The Rising Rhinobots, CAD their initial robot design. We met with their team in the beginning of the season and helped them learn and implement CAD (Fusion 360) in the design of their robot. We also mentored team DreaMKEepers during the Mentor Madness scrimmage. We helped them with their game strategy and mechanical design as they entered their first competition. In the future, we also plan on using our custom built MPCNC project to help local teams custom fabricate parts.
FIRST Mentoring (Dedicated ~250 hours)
CAD Outreach & Mentoring (Invested ~86 hours)
Our design process
We created  separate CAD outreach team that was in charge of this specific program. This sub-team included team members with prior experience in CAD, and a few new team members that were interested in CAD. To build a common knowledge base, every sub-team member took an online CAD course so that they get an idea of the basic and complex ideas of CAD. After that we develop a curriculum containing different CAD ideas and concepts  with projects that students could make in Fusion 360 software .
[IMAGE #138: Google Shape;3765;p14]
[IMAGE #139: Google Shape;3766;p14]
In the past, individual teams have reached out to us for CAD help. While we were able to hold outreach events and help them learn CAD,  we realized that there was a lack of CAD knowledge in our community. Many teams had great mechanical and software knowledge but were unable to put those skills to use. Since we have team members very experienced in CAD, we decided to start an outreach program dedicated to teaching teams CAD.
    Our Impact
We did 4 different outreach events with 4 FLL and FTC teams impacting  over 40 students. We intend to continue this effort on an ongoing basis annually.
The teams and kids who have completed our course are now  able to use the  CAD and Fusion 360 skills we taught them to develop robot parts with complex features. This will elevate the Robot designs for many teams in Wisconsin.
We were also able to personally gain from this experience because we made special connections to our community. Also,  by educating students interested in CAD, we refined our CAD knowledge and gained real world teaching experience.

Vijay Sankaran - Vice President and CTO of Johnson Controls 
We were very fortunate this year to have the chance to host the CTO of Johnson Controls, Mr. Vijay Sankaran. We hosted a zoom webinar and invited all Wisconsin FTC and FRC to join. Due to the theme of FIRST Energize, we got the chance to learn about Johnson Control’s work in Energy Sustainability. Mr. Sankaran also taught us the methods and processes of Disruptive Innovation. We were incredibly fortunate to host such a prolific and impactful speaker. 
Ashish Udani - Senior Applications Engineer at Midwest Engineered Systems
We planned and hosted a session with Mr. Ashish Udani—a professional with more than 30 years of robotics career experience—to which we invited all Elmbrook FIRST teams. We learned about the application of robotics in the engineering world and career opportunities in automation. 
Mentor Madness
Burleigh Elementary FLL Expo/ Scrimmage
EEF Explore STEAM Event
We connected with sponsors and school district families through the Elmbrook Education Foundation’s Explore STEAM event. Along with the other 4 FTC teams in our school district, we promoted FIRST to ~490 guests (300 elementary and middle school students + parents). We demoed and displayed our robot iterations and MPCNC machine & led other STEM activities.
Maker Faire Milwaukee
As a team, we wanted to actively engage with the engineering community. Leveraging our community connections at Northwestern Mutual, we hosted a booth at Maker Faire Milwaukee and spread the word about FIRST and FTC to interested engineers and creators. We showcased our custom MPCNC to teach kids about fabrication and robot design. We met Jim Seymour, CTO at E-Blox, to select kits for our hospital outreach project. 
Additional Volunteering Efforts
Our team makes a constant effort to help at FIRST events. We volunteered  at 5  events & qualifiers, notably, the FLL Franklin Regional Qualifier and the FTC Battle of Brookfield Qualifier.

We were invited to the Mentor Madness Scrimmage and assigned to Mentor FTC team 21748 the dreaMKEepers. We mentored them in improving the efficiency of their robot design and software capabilities.

=== SLIDE 15 ===
[layout: 2_Title and Content - No Rule]
Outreach: Closing the Technology Divide for Hundreds/Yr
We started this project with impact in the local Milwaukee area, and this year we expanded internationally. 
This project provides tech access to hundreds of students/year 
We have already provided 25 laptops (and have more ready for teams in need for the 2023-24 season!)
	10 to FLL teams
	5 to FTC teams 
	5 to STEAM Milwaukee for classroom Learning Labs
	5 to República School in Honduras
We have made this an annual, sustainable project with increasing impact every year. This year we extended our outreach internationally by providing 5 laptops with bilingual STEM software installed to an underprivileged community in Honduras, to enable more and more kids to experience STEM.
Our effort was even covered on Dec 25th, 2021, on the CBS 58 News channel: “Robotics Team Closes the Technology Divide.”
Our outreach project has become so popular that teams and STEM organizations have started reaching out to us asking for laptops, and companies have offered to donate their used devices in the future! 
Why
Doing a few things better | Focus | Determination | Impact
Problem Statement: While interacting with some talented kids from underprivileged school districts in Milwaukee, we realized their access to FIRST/STEM is inhibited due to a lack of access to basics like computers and mentoring.  
We organized 11 computer collection events at schools and libraries: and collected 125 devices. 
We inventoried and tracked all devices through a detailed, well-documented process to identify items for refurbishing/repair, recycling, or selling.
We learned to responsibly refurbish computers and clean up data on the hard drive, fix hardware issues, and install new operating systems and applications.
Planning & Learning
CONNECTing with our community
To collect the computers, we organized 11 computer collection events with our school districts and local public libraries.
We reached out to our school district’s IT team who helped us learn the IT skills needed to responsibly refurbish the computers and help us get past any roadblocks we hit.
We secured low-cost, authorized Microsoft software licenses through the World’s Largest Microsoft Authorized Refurbisher (MAR), Blair Tech, to activate windows 10 pro when needed.
We then found homes for the laptops through organizations like Boys & Girls Club of Milwaukee, MSOE, STEM Forward, and Medical College of WI (Honduras mission).
Connections Made + Our Process
Solution:  We wanted to focus on the most important issue – access to computers. We collected, refurbished & donated 25 computers to FIRST Teams, and STEM organizations and also expanded our impact internationally to help a school in Honduras.

=== SLIDE 16 ===
[layout: 2_Title and Content - No Rule]
Outreach: Connecting Kids to STEAM and FIRST
Hospital Outreach Project
The  hospital outreach project has been one of the most meaningful we have ever done.
Doing a few things better | Focus | Determination | Impact
Problem Statement : 
As kids who have grown up in Brookfield, we have had so many opportunities to experience STEAM and FIRST in the community. 
Solution :  We could help these kids by giving them opportunities to learn about STEAM and FIRST through fun sessions where they build/interact with different STEAM and robotics kits. For the first session we decided to use E-Blox Lights N’ Motion kits. Once the kids build the kits, they will also learn about how it all works. This gives these kids an opportunity many of them have never had, and maybe they will discover a new interest that will keep them engaged. There has also been a lot of medical research of how robotics/STEAM during treatment for kids with cancer increases their general well-being, and hopefully this project will do just that.
Approach:  We followed a plan 
First, we reached out to physicians and Child-Life Specialists at Children's Hospital of Wisconsin who were willing to learn about our team and idea. We asked them questions about constraints, requirements, expectations, etc. 
We then created a proposal, which laid out the basic agenda for our first session and information about the kit. The specialists gave feedback on it.
Then initially ordered 20 E-Blox Lights and Motion kits and sanitized them.  (It has been popular so we bought 5 more!)
For in-person patients, we created presentations and led them through how the kit works, talked about the FIRST experience, and shared information on how to join the FIRST community. (We tested our in-person training class with an FLL team and gathered feedback before delivering it to patients.)
For virtual patients, we created a tutorial video so they could learn when it was convenient or when they felt better.
The Impact is just beginning!    
We have delivered 2 in-person sessions and had  21 patients use our kits and training video already.  We are the first robotics team to be invited to host a full series of STEAM Workshops to inpatients at Children’s Hospital and are scheduled for 5 more before December. 
At the suggestion of Children’s specialists, we also planned a STEM Fair for Childhood Illness Survivors.  We may further expand to outpatient clinics and camps in the summer. We will likely bring in FLL and FTC robots in future sessions. The kids will be able to program it, watch it perform missions, collaborate, and have fun.
Results in the Community
At our first 2 sessions the kids who were present all rated it 4-5 stars and would love to do it again. Many of the parents there, were also very interested in FIRST, and how to join.  We are very glad we were able to show these kids new opportunities and experiences they can use in the future. They are going through something unimaginably hard, and anything we can do to help them smile and feel excited and hopeful, we will do our best to do. We are excited to be providing “play therapy!”
CONNECTing with our community
[IMAGE #176: Google Shape;3880;p16]
[IMAGE #177: Google Shape;3881;p16]
[IMAGE #178: Google Shape;3882;p16]
[IMAGE #179: Google Shape;3883;p16]
[IMAGE #180: Google Shape;3884;p16]
15
However, for kids battling cancer who have been in and out of hospitals due to treatments and check-ups, they haven’t had experiences like that. We learned about the benefits of “play therapy,” and decided to bring them joy and relief  through STEAM!
Planned & Delivered 3 Accessible Formats
(Invested ~260 hours for Inpatient & Outpatient)
```

```text
What worked
The arm could reach all three pole heights.
Mecanum wheels allowed for the freedom of movement in many directions.
The various parts of the robot we used were nice fits, creating a strong robot.
Key Leanings 
Learned never to make something overly complex.
We learned to consider qualities of design like center of gravity before building in order to keep the build process efficient. 
[IMAGE #72: Google Shape;2869;p8]
What worked
Robot was able to pick up knocked down cones on the field
Efficient driver control with the autonomous software elements implemented
Key Learnings 
The active intake mechanism was way too complicated and unreliable
Robot is too clunky and heavy to compete with fast robots
We need a cone scoring system that is more reliable and less prone to failures like jamming
Lighter elevator design is needed
```
